献给

谨献者

E. 内斯比特
于威尔庄园，

肯特郡埃尔塔姆，1910 年。

## I The Beginning / 起点

===Original===
Philip Haldane and his sister lived in a little red-roofed house in a little red-roofed town. They had a little garden and a little balcony, and a little stable with a little pony in it﻿—and a little cart for the pony to draw; a little canary hung in a little cage in the little bow-window, and the neat little servant kept everything as bright and clean as a little new pin.

Philip had no one but his sister, and she had no one but Philip. Their parents were dead, and Helen, who was twenty years older than Philip and was really his half-sister, was all the mother he had ever known. And he had never envied other boys their mothers, because Helen was so kind and clever and dear. She gave up almost all her time to him; she taught him all the lessons he learned; she played with him, inventing the most wonderful new games and adventures. So that every morning when Philip woke he knew that he was waking to a new day of joyous and interesting happenings. And this went on till Philip was ten years old, and he had no least shadow of a doubt that it would go on forever. The beginning of the change came one day when he and Helen had gone for a picnic to the wood where the waterfall was, and as they were driving back behind the stout old pony, who was so good and quiet that Philip was allowed to drive it. They were coming up the last lane before the turning where their house was, and Helen said:

“Tomorrow we’ll weed the aster bed and have tea in the garden.”

“Jolly,” said Philip, and they turned the corner and came in sight of their white little garden gate. And a man was coming out of it﻿—a man who was not one of the friends they both knew. He turned and came to meet them. Helen put her hand on the reins﻿—a thing which she had always taught Philip was *never* done﻿—and the pony stopped. The man, who was, as Philip put it to himself, “tall and tweedy,” came across in front of the pony’s nose and stood close by the wheel on the side where Helen sat. She shook hands with him, and said, “How do you do?” in quite the usual way. But after that they whispered. Whispered! And Philip knew how rude it is to whisper, because Helen had often told him this. He heard one or two words, “at last,” and “over now,” and “this evening, then.”

===Chinese===
菲利普·霍尔丹（Philip Haldane）和姐姐住在一座红瓦顶小镇里一座红瓦顶的小房子里。他们有一座小花园和一个小阳台，还有一个小马厩，里面养着一匹小马——还有一辆让小马拉的小车；一只小金丝雀关在凸窗里的小笼子里，那整洁的小女仆把样样东西都收拾得像崭新的小别针一样又亮又净。

菲利普除了姐姐没有别人，姐姐除了菲利普也没有别人。他们的父母都不在了。海伦（Helen）比菲利普大二十岁，其实是他同父异母的姐姐，却一直是他所认识的唯一的母亲。他也从不羡慕别的孩子有母亲，因为海伦是那么善良、那么聪明、那么亲爱。她几乎把全部时间都给了他；他学的功课全是她教的；她陪他玩，发明出最奇妙的新游戏和新冒险。于是每天早晨菲利普一醒来，就知道等待自己的是充满欢乐和趣事的新的一天。这样的日子一直过到菲利普十岁，他心里没有一丝一毫的怀疑，只当它会永远这样过下去。变化开始的那一天，他和海伦去有瀑布的树林里野餐，回程时赶着那匹结实的老小马——它又乖又稳，所以允许菲利普亲自驾驭。他们正走上回家转弯前的最后一条小路，海伦说：

「明天我们把紫菀花坛里的草拔一拔，然后在花园里喝茶。」

「好极了。」菲利普说。他们转过街角，望见自家白色的小院门。一个男人正从门里出来——一个不属于他们共同的朋友圈子的男人。他转过身来迎上前。海伦把手按在了缰绳上——她一向教导菲利普，这事是绝对做不得的——小马停了下来。那男人——用菲利普心里的话说，「高高大大，一身花呢」——从小马鼻子前面绕过来，站在海伦坐的那一侧的车轮旁边。她和他握了手，用再平常不过的口气说了声「你好」。可接下来他们竟咬起了耳朵。咬耳朵！菲利普知道咬耳朵有多没礼貌，因为海伦常常这么告诉他。他听到了一两个词，「总算」，还有「都过去了」，还有「那就今晚」。

===Original===
After that Helen said, “This is my brother Philip,” and the man shook hands with him﻿—across Helen, another thing which Philip knew was not manners, and said, “I hope we shall be the best of friends.” Pip said, “How do you do?” because that is the polite thing to say. But inside himself he said, “I don’t want to be friends with *you*.”

Then the man took off his hat and walked away, and Philip and his sister went home. She seemed different, somehow, and he was sent to bed a little earlier than usual, but he could not go to sleep for a long time, because he heard the front-door bell ring and afterwards a man’s voice and Helen’s going on and on in the little drawing-room under the room which was his bedroom. He went to sleep at last, and when he woke up in the morning it was raining, and the sky was grey and miserable. He lost his collar-stud, he tore one of his stockings as he pulled it on, he pinched his finger in the door, and he dropped his tooth-mug, with water in it too, and the mug was broken and the water went into his boots. There are mornings, you know, when things happen like that. This was one of them.

Then he went down to breakfast, which tasted not quite so nice as usual. He was late, of course. The bacon fat was growing grey with waiting for him, as Helen said, in the cheerful voice that had always said all the things he liked best to hear. But Philip didn’t smile. It did not seem the sort of morning for smiling, and the grey rain beat against the window.

After breakfast Helen said, “Tea in the garden is indefinitely postponed, and it’s too wet for lessons.”

That was one of her charming ideas﻿—that wet days should not be made worse by lessons.

===Chinese===
之后海伦说：「这是我弟弟菲利普。」那男人越过海伦和他握了手——又一件菲利普知道不合规矩的事——还说：「希望我们能成为最好的朋友。」皮普（Pip）说了声「你好」，因为礼貌上就该这么说。可他心里说的是：「我才不要和*你*做朋友。」

然后那男人戴上帽子走了，菲利普和姐姐回了家。她看上去有些说不出的不一样。他被打发上床的时间比平时早了一点，却久久睡不着，因为他听见前门的门铃响，随后是一个男人的声音和海伦的声音，在他卧室楼下的小客厅里响个不停。他终于睡着了，早上醒来时天正下着雨，天色灰蒙蒙的，凄凄惨惨。他丢了领扣，套长袜时撕开了一道口子，手指让门夹了一下，又把漱口杯掉在地上——杯里还盛着水——杯子摔碎了，水流进了靴子里。你知道的，有些早晨事情就是这样接二连三地出。这天早晨正是如此。

然后他下楼吃早饭，早饭的味道不像平常那么好。他当然迟到了。培根上的油都等得发灰了——海伦用她那快活的嗓音这么说，那嗓音一向说的都是他最爱听的话。可菲利普没有笑。这看上去不是个适合微笑的早晨，灰色的雨点正敲打着窗子。

早饭后海伦说：「花园茶会无限期推迟，这天气也没法上课。」

这是她的迷人主意之一——下雨天不该再让功课雪上加霜。

===Original===
“What shall we do?” she said; “shall we talk about the island? Shall I make another map of it? And put in all the gardens and fountains and swings?”

The island was a favourite play. Somewhere in the warm seas where palm trees are, and rainbow-coloured sands, the island was said to be﻿—their own island, beautified by their fancy with everything they liked and wanted, and Philip was never tired of talking about it. There were times when he almost believed that the island was real. He was king of the island and Helen was queen, and no one else was to be allowed on it. Only these two.

But this morning even the thought of the island failed to charm. Philip straggled away to the window and looked out dismally at the soaked lawn and the dripping laburnum trees, and the row of raindrops hanging fat and full on the iron gate.

“What is it, Pippin?” Helen asked. “Don’t tell me you’re going to have horrid measles, or red-hot scarlet fever, or noisy whooping-cough.”

She came across and laid her hand on his forehead.

“Why, you’re quite hot, boy of my heart. Tell sister, what is it?”

===Chinese===
「我们干什么好呢？」她说，「说说那座岛怎么样？要不要我再画一张岛图？把所有的花园、喷泉和秋千都画进去？」

梦想之岛（the island）是他们最爱玩的一种游戏。据说它坐落在温暖海洋的某处，那里有棕榈树和彩虹色的沙滩——那是他们自己的岛，被他们的幻想装点上了他们喜欢和想要的一切，菲利普永远也聊不腻。有时候他几乎相信那座岛是真的。他是岛上的国王，海伦是王后，谁也不许上岛。只许他们两个。

可是这天早晨，连想想那座岛也提不起劲来。菲利普蔫头耷脑地蹭到窗前，凄凉地望着泡透了的草坪、滴着水的金链花树，还有一串胖鼓鼓、沉甸甸的雨珠挂在铁栅门上。

「怎么啦，皮平（Pippin）？」海伦问，「别告诉我你要出讨厌的麻疹，或者滚烫的猩红热，或者吵闹的百日咳。」

她走过来，把手贴在他的额头上。

「哎呀，你挺烫的呢，我的心肝宝贝。告诉姐姐，怎么啦？」

===Original===
“*You* tell *me*,” said Philip slowly.

“Tell you what, Pip?”

“You think you ought to bear it alone, like in books, and be noble and all that. But you *must* tell me; you promised you’d never have any secrets from me, Helen, you know you did.”

Helen put her arm round him and said nothing. And from her silence Pip drew the most desperate and harrowing conclusions. The silence lasted. The rain gurgled in the water-pipe and dripped on the ivy. The canary in the green cage that hung in the window put its head on one side and tweaked a seed husk out into Philip’s face, then twittered defiantly. But his sister said nothing.

“Don’t,” said Philip suddenly, “don’t break it to me; tell me straight out.”

“Tell you what?” she said again.

===Chinese===
「该*你*告诉*我*。」菲利普慢慢地说。

「告诉你什么，皮普？」

「你以为该像书里写的那样一个人扛着，还要显得高尚什么的。可你*必须*告诉我；你保证过永远不对我保守秘密的，海伦，你知道你保证过。」

海伦搂住他，一言不发。皮普从她的沉默里得出了最绝望、最揪心的结论。沉默持续着。雨水在雨水管里咕噜咕噜地响，滴在常春藤上。挂在窗前绿笼子里的金丝雀歪着头，把一粒啄空的籽壳甩到菲利普脸上，然后挑衅似的啾了一声。可姐姐还是什么都没说。

「别，」菲利普突然说，「别这么一点点透露给我；直说吧。」

「告诉你什么？」她又说。

===Original===
“What is it?” he said. “*I* know how these unforetold misfortunes happen. Someone always comes﻿—and then it’s broken to the family.”

“*What* is?” she asked.

“The misfortune,” said Philip breathlessly. “Oh, Helen, I’m not a baby. Do tell me! Have we lost our money in a burst bank? Or is the landlord going to put bailiffs into our furniture? Or are we going to be falsely accused about forgery, or being burglars?”

All the books Philip had ever read worked together in his mind to produce these melancholy suggestions. Helen laughed, and instantly felt a stiffening withdrawal of her brother from her arm.

“No, no, my Pippin, dear,” she made haste to say. “Nothing horrid like that has happened.”

“Then what is it?” he asked, with a growing impatience that felt like a wolf gnawing inside him.

===Chinese===
「到底是什么事？」他说。「*我*可知道这种飞来横祸是怎么来的。总有人上门——然后家里人就被一点点告知了。」

「*什么*事？」她问。

「横祸呗，」菲利普上气不接下气地说。「哦，海伦，我不是小娃娃了。快告诉我！我们的钱是不是存进倒闭的银行打了水漂？还是房东要派法警来查封我们的家具？还是我们要被诬陷伪造文书，或者被当成夜盗贼？」

菲利普读过的所有书在他脑子里一起使劲，造出了这些愁云惨雾的猜想。海伦笑了，随即感觉到弟弟从她搂着的手臂里一下子僵着抽了出去。

「不，不，我的皮平，亲爱的，」她赶紧说。「没发生那种可怕的事。」

「那到底是什么？」他问，火气越来越旺，那感觉像一只狼在他心里啃咬。

===Original===
“I didn’t want to tell you all in a hurry like this,” she said anxiously; “but don’t you worry, my boy of boys. It’s something that makes me very happy. I hope it will you, too.”

He swung round in the circling of her arm and looked at her with sudden ecstasy.

“Oh, Helen, dear﻿—I know! Someone has left you a hundred thousand pounds a year﻿—someone you once opened a railway-carriage door for﻿—and now I can have a pony of my very own to ride. Can’t I?”

“Yes,” said Helen slowly, “you can have a pony; but nobody’s left me anything. Look here, my Pippin,” she added, very quickly, “don’t ask any more questions. I’ll tell you. When I was quite little like you I had a dear friend I used to play with all day long, and when we grew up we were friends still. He lived quite near us. And then he married someone else. And then the someone died. And now he wants me to marry him. And he’s got lots of horses and a beautiful house and park,” she added.

“And where shall I be?” he asked.

“With me, of course, wherever I am.”

===Chinese===
「我不想这么急急忙忙地告诉你，」她不安地说，「不过你别担心，我的宝贝中的宝贝。是件让我非常非常高兴的事。我希望它也会让你高兴。」

他在她搂着的手臂里猛地转过身来，狂喜地望着她。

「哦，海伦，亲爱的——我知道了！有人留给你一年十万英镑——你从前帮人开过一次火车车厢门的那个人——现在我可以有一匹完全属于我自己的小马骑了。对不对？」

「对，」海伦缓缓地说，「你可以有小马；不过并没有人留给我什么东西。听我说，我的皮平，」她很快地补了一句，「别再问了。我告诉你。我像你这么小的时候，有一位亲爱的朋友，我们成天成天地一起玩，长大以后我们也还是朋友。他住得离我们家很近。后来他娶了别人。再后来那个别人死了。现在他想让我嫁给他。他有好些马，还有一栋漂亮的房子和一座园林。」她补充道。

「那我呢？」他问。

「当然跟我在一起，我在哪儿，你就在哪儿。」

===Original===
“It won’t be just us two any more, though,” said Philip, “and you said it should be, forever and ever.”

“But I didn’t know then, Pip, dear. He’s been wanting me so long﻿—”

“Don’t *I* want you?” said Pip to himself.

“And he’s got a little girl that you’ll like so to play with,” she went on. “Her name’s Lucy, and she’s just a year younger than you. And you’ll be the greatest friends with her. And you’ll both have ponies to ride, and﻿—”

“I hate her,” cried Philip, very loud, “and I hate him, and I hate their beastly ponies. And I hate *you*!” And with these dreadful words he flung off her arm and rushed out of the room, banging the door after him﻿—on purpose.

Well, she found him in the boot-cupboard, among the gaiters and goloshes and cricket-stumps and old rackets, and they kissed and cried and hugged each other, and he said he was sorry he had been naughty. But in his heart that was the only thing he was sorry for. He was sorry that he had made Helen unhappy. He still hated “that man,” and most of all he hated Lucy.

===Chinese===
「可那就不再只是我们两个了，」菲利普说，「你说过永远永远都只我们两个的。」

「可那时候我不知道啊，皮普，亲爱的。他盼我盼了这么久——」

「难道*我*不盼你吗？」皮普在心里说。

「他有个小姑娘，你会特别乐意跟她玩的，」她接着说。「她叫露西（Lucy），刚好比你小一岁。你会跟她成为顶好顶好的朋友。你们俩都会有小马骑，还有——」

「我恨她，」菲利普大声喊，「我恨他，我恨他们那些讨厌的小马。我还恨*你*！」说完这句可怕的话，他甩开她的手臂冲出房间，故意把门砰地摔上。

后来，她在放绑腿套、橡胶套鞋、板球门柱和旧球拍的靴柜里找到了他，他们又亲又哭又搂，他说对不起，他刚才不乖了。可是在心里，他只为这一件事抱歉。他抱歉让海伦难过了。他仍旧恨「那个男人」，而最恨的是露西。

===Original===
He had to be polite to that man. His sister was very fond of that man, and this made Philip hate him still more, while at the same time it made him careful not to show how he hated him. Also it made him feel that hating that man was not quite fair to his sister, whom he loved. But there were no feelings of that kind to come in the way of the detestation he felt for Lucy. Helen had told him that Lucy had fair hair and wore it in two plaits; and he pictured her to himself as a fat, stumpy little girl, exactly like the little girl in the story of “The Sugar Bread” in the old oblong *Shock-Headed Peter* book that had belonged to Helen when she was little.

Helen was quite happy. She divided her love between the boy she loved and the man she was going to marry, and she believed that they were both as happy as she was. The man, whose name was Peter Graham, was happy enough; the boy, who was Philip, was amused﻿—for she kept him so﻿—but under the amusement he was miserable.

And the wedding-day came and went. And Philip travelled on a very hot afternoon by strange trains and a strange carriage to a strange house, where he was welcomed by a strange nurse and﻿—Lucy.

“You won’t mind going to stay at Peter’s beautiful house without me, will you, dear?” Helen had asked. “Everyone will be kind to you, and you’ll have Lucy to play with.”

And Philip said he didn’t mind. What else could he say, without being naughty and making Helen cry again?

===Chinese===
他不得不对那个男人讲礼貌。姐姐非常喜欢那个男人，这让菲利普更加恨他，同时也让他小心着不露出恨意。这也让他觉得，恨那个男人对自己深爱的姐姐不太公平。可是对露西的憎恶，就没有这种顾虑来拦路了。海伦告诉过他，露西有一头金发，梳成两条辫子；他在心里把她想成一个又矮又胖的小姑娘，活像海伦小时候那本旧的长方形《蓬头彼得》（Shock-Headed Peter）书里《糖面包》（The Sugar Bread）那篇故事里的小姑娘。

海伦挺幸福。她把爱分给她爱的男孩和她要嫁的男人，并且相信他们两个都跟她一样幸福。那男人名叫彼得·格雷厄姆（Peter Graham），他够幸福了；那男孩——也就是菲利普——也够开心——因为她变着法儿让他开心——可在开心底下，他苦闷得很。

婚礼的日子来了又过去了。菲利普在一个酷热的下午，坐着陌生的火车、陌生的车厢，来到一栋陌生的房子，在那里迎接他的是一位陌生的保姆——还有露西。

「你不会介意独自留在彼得的漂亮房子里住一阵吧，亲爱的？」海伦事先问过他。「人人都会对你好，你还有露西陪你玩呢。」

菲利普说他不介意。不然他还能说什么呢？总不能不乖、又惹海伦哭一场吧。

===Original===
Lucy was not a bit like the Sugar-Bread child. She had fair hair, it is true, and it was plaited in two braids, but they were very long and straight; she herself was long and lean and had a freckled face and bright, jolly eyes.

“I’m so glad you’ve come,” she said, meeting him on the steps of the most beautiful house he had ever seen; “we can play all sort of things now that you can’t play when you’re only one. I’m an only child,” she added, with a sort of melancholy pride. Then she laughed. “ ‘Only’ rhymes with ‘lonely,’ doesn’t it?” she said.

“I don’t know,” said Philip, with deliberate falseness, for he knew quite well.

He said no more.

Lucy tried two or three other beginnings of conversation, but Philip contradicted everything she said.

“I’m afraid he’s very very stupid,” she said to her nurse, an extremely trained nurse, who firmly agreed with her. And when her aunt came to see her next day, Lucy said that the little new boy was stupid, and disagreeable as well as stupid, and Philip confirmed this opinion of his behaviour to such a degree that the aunt, who was young and affectionate, had Lucy’s clothes packed at once and carried her off for a few days’ visit.

===Chinese===
露西一点也不像《糖面包》里的孩子。她确实有金发，也确实编成两条辫子，可那两条辫子又长又直；她本人也又高又瘦，脸上有雀斑，一双眼睛又亮又快活。

「你来了我真高兴，」她在台阶上迎着他说——那是他见过的最漂亮的房子；「现在我们可以玩各种各样的东西了，只有一个人的时候可玩不成。我是个独生女，」她带着一点忧郁的骄傲补充说。接着她笑了。「『only』（独生）和『lonely』（孤单）是押韵的，不是吗？」她说。

「我不知道。」菲利普说，故意撒了个谎，其实他清楚得很。

他没再多说一个字。

露西又试着起了两三次话头，可菲利普把她说的每句话都顶了回去。

「我看他是蠢得厉害，」她对她的保姆说——那是一位受过极其专业训练的保姆，坚定地赞同她的看法。第二天她姑妈来看她时，露西说新来的小男孩不但蠢，还难缠，而菲利普用自己的表现把这一评价证实得如此到位，以致那位年轻又热心肠的姑妈当即叫人收拾了露西的衣裳，带她去别人家小住几天了。

===Original===
So Philip and the nurse were left at the Grange. There was nobody else in the house but servants. And now Philip began to know what loneliness meant. The letters and the picture postcards which his sister sent every day from the odd towns on the continent of Europe, which she visited on her honeymoon, did not cheer the boy. They merely exasperated him, reminding him of the time when she was all his own, and was too near to him to need to send him postcards and letters.

The extremely trained nurse, who wore a grey uniform and white cap and apron, disapproved of Philip to the depths of her well-disciplined nature. “Cantankerous little pig,” she called him to herself.

To the housekeeper she said, “He is an unusually difficult and disagreeable child. I should imagine that his education has been much neglected. He wants a tight hand.”

She did not use a tight hand to him, however. She treated him with an indifference more annoying than tyranny. He had immense liberty of a desolate, empty sort. The great house was his to go to and fro in. But he was not allowed to touch anything in it. The garden was his﻿—to wander through, but he must not pluck flowers or fruit. He had no lessons, it is true; but, then, he had no games either. There was a nursery, but he was not imprisoned in it﻿—was not even encouraged to spend his time there. He was sent out for walks, and alone, for the park was large and safe. And the nursery was the room of all that great house that attracted him most, for it was full of toys of the most fascinating kind. A rocking-horse as big as a pony, the finest dolls’ house you ever saw, boxes of tea-things, boxes of bricks﻿—both the wooden and the terra-cotta sorts﻿—puzzle maps, dominoes, chessmen, draughts, every kind of toy or game that you have ever had or ever wished to have.

And Pip was not allowed to play with any of them.

“You mustn’t touch anything, if you please,” the nurse said, with that icy politeness which goes with a uniform. “The toys are Miss Lucy’s. No; I couldn’t be responsible for giving you permission to play with them. No; I couldn’t think of troubling Miss Lucy by writing to ask her if you may play with them. No; I couldn’t take upon myself to give you Miss Lucy’s address.”

===Chinese===
于是菲利普和保姆留在了格兰奇庄园（the Grange）。屋里除了仆人再没有别人。这时菲利普开始尝到孤独是什么滋味。姐姐每天从欧洲大陆那些古怪的城镇寄来的信和图画明信片——她正在那些地方度蜜月——一点也提不起男孩的精神。它们只是让他恼火，提醒他想起从前她完全属于他、近在身边根本用不着给他寄明信片和信的日子。

那位受过极其专业训练的保姆，穿灰色制服，戴白帽子和白围裙，用她那训练有素的天性最深处嫌弃着菲利普。「爱抬杠的小猪猡。」她心里这样叫他。

她对女管家说：「这孩子格外难缠、格外讨人嫌。我猜他的教育被大大耽误了。他欠一顿严管。」

然而她并没有对他施以严管。她用一种比专横更气人的冷漠对待他。他享有的是一种空空荡荡、无边无际的自由。这座大宅子任他走来走去，可里面的东西一概不许碰。花园归他——可以随便逛，但花果不许摘。他确实不用上课，可他也同样没的玩。有一间育儿室，倒也没把他关在里面——甚至不鼓励他待在里头。他被打发出去散步，独自一人，因为园子又大又安全。而育儿室是整座大宅里最吸引他的房间，因为里面全是好玩得不得了的玩具。一匹小马那么大的摇木马，你从没见过的最漂亮的玩具小屋，一盒盒茶具、一盒盒积木——木头的和陶的都有——拼图地图、多米诺骨牌、国际象棋棋子、跳棋，还有各种你有过或梦想过的玩具和游戏。

而皮普一样也不许玩。

「请你别碰任何东西，」保姆说，带着制服特有的那种冷冰冰的客气。「玩具是露西小姐的。不，我可担待不起、不能准你玩它们。不，我可不敢麻烦露西小姐、写信问你能不能玩。不，我可不能自作主张把露西小姐的地址给你。」

===Original===
For Philip’s boredom and his desire had humbled him even to the asking for this.

For two whole days he lived at the Grange, hating it and everyone in it; for the servants took their cue from the nurse, and the child felt that in the whole house he had not a friend. Somehow he had got the idea firmly in his head that this was a time when Helen was not to be bothered about anything; so he wrote to her that he was quite well, thank you, and the park was very pretty and Lucy had lots of nice toys. He felt very brave and noble, and like a martyr. And he set his teeth to bear it all. It was like spending a few days at the dentist’s.

And then suddenly everything changed. The nurse got a telegram. A brother who had been thought to be drowned at sea had abruptly come home. She must go to see him. “If it costs me the situation,” she said to the housekeeper, who answered:

“Oh, well﻿—go, then. I’ll be responsible for the boy﻿—sulky little brat.”

And the nurse went. In a happy bustle she packed her boxes and went. At the last moment Philip, on the doorstep watching her climb into the dogcart, suddenly sprang forward.

===Chinese===
因为菲利普实在闷得慌、馋得慌，居然落到开口讨这个的地步。

他在格兰奇庄园整整熬了两天，恨着这里和这里的每一个人；因为仆人们都看保姆的脸色行事，孩子觉得整座宅子里自己没有一个朋友。不知怎么，他脑子里牢牢认定了这是海伦不便被任何事打扰的非常时期；于是他写信给她说自己很好，谢谢关心，园子很漂亮，露西有好多好多好玩具。他觉得自己又勇敢又高尚，像个殉道者。他咬紧牙关熬着这一切。就像在牙医那里一连住上几天。

后来，突然间一切都变了。保姆收到一封电报。一个本以为葬身大海的兄弟突然回家了。她必须去看看他。「哪怕丢了这份差事，」她对女管家说。女管家回答：

「哦，那就——去吧。孩子我来负责——这绷着脸的小坏蛋。」

于是保姆走了。她喜气洋洋、手忙脚乱地收拾了箱子就走。最后一刻，菲利普站在门口台阶上，望着她爬上双轮马车，猛地冲上前去。
===Original===
“Oh, Nurse!” he cried, blundering against the almost moving wheel, and it was the first time he had called her by any name. “Nurse, do﻿—do say I may take Lucy’s toys to play with; it *is* so lonely here. I may, mayn’t I? I may take them?”

Perhaps the nurse’s heart was softened by her own happiness and the thought of the brother who was not drowned. Perhaps she was only in such a hurry that she did not know what she was saying. At any rate, when Philip said for the third time, “May I take them?” she hastily answered:

“Bless the child! Take anything you like. Mind the wheel, for goodness’ sake. Goodbye, everybody!” waved her hand to the servants assembled at the top of the wide steps, and was whirled off to joyous reunion with the undrowned brother.

Philip drew a deep breath of satisfaction, went straight up to the nursery, took out all the toys, and examined every single one of them. It took him all the afternoon.

The next day he looked at all the things again and longed to make something with them. He was accustomed to the joy that comes of making things. He and Helen had built many a city for the dream island out of his own two boxes of bricks and certain other things in the house﻿—her Japanese cabinet, the dominoes and chessmen, cardboard boxes, books, the lids of kettles and teapots. But they had never had enough bricks. Lucy had enough bricks for anything.

===Chinese===
「哦，保姆！」他喊道，踉跄着扑向那几乎已经在转动的车轮——这是他头一回用任何称呼叫她。「保姆，求——求你说一声我可以拿露西的玩具玩；这里实在太孤单了。我可以的，对不对？我可以拿它们吧？」

也许是保姆的心被她自己的喜事和那位没淹死的兄弟软化了。也许她只是匆忙得不知道自己在说什么。总之，当菲利普第三次说「我可以拿吗」的时候，她匆匆答道：

「老天保佑这孩子！喜欢什么就拿什么好了。看在老天的份上，当心车轮。再见啦，各位！」她朝聚在大台阶顶端的仆人们挥了挥手，一阵风似的走了，去和那位没淹死的兄弟欢乐团聚。

菲利普心满意足地深深吸了一口气，径直上楼到育儿室，把玩具统统拿了出来，一件一件地查看。这花了他整整一个下午。

第二天他把所有东西又看了一遍，手痒得真想拿它们做点什么。他习惯了亲手做东西的那种快乐。他和海伦用他自己那两盒积木和家里的一些别的东西——她的日本橱柜、多米诺骨牌和棋子、纸盒、书、水壶盖和茶壶盖——为梦想之岛建过好多座城。可他们从来没有够用的积木。露西的积木要多多有多少。

===Original===
He began to build a city on the nursery table. But to build with bricks alone is poor work when you have been used to building with all sorts of other things.

“It looks like a factory,” said Philip discontentedly. He swept the building down and replaced the bricks in their different boxes.

“There must be something downstairs that would come in useful,” he told himself, “and she did say, ‘Take what you like.’ ”

By armfuls, two and three at a time, he carried down the boxes of bricks and the boxes of blocks, the draughts, the chessmen, and the box of dominoes. He took them into the long drawing-room where the crystal chandeliers were, and the chairs covered in brown holland﻿—and the many long, light windows, and the cabinets and tables covered with the most interesting things.

He cleared a big writing-table of such useless and unimportant objects as blotting-pad, silver inkstand, and red-backed books, and there was a clear space for his city.

===Chinese===
他开始在育儿室的桌子上建一座城。可是当你习惯了用各种各样别的东西来建造，光用积木就未免寒碜。

「看着像座工厂。」菲利普不满地说。他把建筑一扫而倒，把积木分门别类装回了各个盒子。

「楼下一定有能用得上的东西，」他对自己说，「何况她确实说过，『喜欢什么就拿什么』。」

他一次抱上两三盒，来来回回把积木盒和积木块盒、跳棋、国际象棋棋子和那盒多米诺骨牌都搬了下去。他把它们搬进那间长长的客厅，那里有水晶枝形吊灯、罩着棕色亚麻布套的椅子——还有许多扇长而明亮的窗，还有摆满最有趣物件的橱柜和桌子。

他把吸墨纸垫、银墨水台和红皮面的书这些无用又不紧要的东西从一张大写字台上清走，他的城有了一块开阔的地盘。

===Original===
He began to build.

A bronze Egyptian god on a black and gold cabinet seemed to be looking at him from across the room.

“All right,” said Philip. “I’ll build you a temple. You wait a bit.”

The bronze god waited and the temple grew, and two silver candlesticks, topped by chessmen, served admirably as pillars for the portico. He made a journey to the nursery to fetch the Noah’s Ark animals﻿—the pair of elephants, each standing on a brick, flanked the entrance. It looked splendid, like an Assyrian temple in the pictures Helen had shown him. But the bricks, wherever he built with them alone, looked mean, and like factories or workhouses. Bricks alone always do.

Philip explored again. He found the library. He made several journeys. He brought up twenty-seven volumes bound in white vellum with marbled boards, a set of Shakespeare, ten volumes in green morocco. These made pillars and cloisters, dark, mysterious, and attractive. More Noah’s Ark animals added an Egyptian-looking finish to the building.

“Lor’, ain’t it pretty!” said the parlourmaid, who came to call him to tea. “You are clever with your fingers, Master Philip, I will say that for you. But you’ll catch it, taking all them things.”

===Chinese===
他开始建造。

黑金相间的橱柜上，一尊青铜的埃及神像好像正隔着房间看他。

「好吧，」菲利普说，「我给你建座神庙。你稍等。」

青铜神像等着，神庙渐渐长了起来，两支顶着棋子的银烛台出色地充当了门廊的柱子。他又跑了一趟育儿室去取诺亚方舟（Noah’s Ark）里的动物——那对大象一头踩一块积木，分立在入口两侧。看上去气派极了，像海伦给他看过的图画里的亚述神庙。可是积木，凡是他只用积木搭的地方，看着都寒酸，像工厂或者济贫院。光用积木搭总是这样。

菲利普又出去探察了一番。他发现了图书室。他跑了好几趟。他搬来二十七卷白色羔皮纸封面、云纹纸板的书，一套莎士比亚全集，十卷绿摩洛哥皮面的。这些做了柱子和回廊，幽暗、神秘、诱人。更多的诺亚方舟动物给建筑添上了一层埃及式的收尾。

「哎哟，真俊！」来叫他喝茶的客厅女仆说。「你这双手真巧，菲利普少爷，这话我可得说。不过你把这么多东西都搬出来，回头有你好受的。」

===Original===
“That grey nurse said I might,” said Philip, “and it doesn’t hurt things building with them. My sister and I always did it at home,” he added, looking confidingly at the parlourmaid. She had praised his building. And it was the first time he had mentioned his sister to anyone in that house.

“Well, it’s as good as a peepshow,” said the parlourmaid; “it’s just like them picture postcards my brother in India sends me. All them pillars and domes and things﻿—and the animals too. I don’t know how you fare to think of such things, that I don’t.”

Praise is sweet. He slipped his hand into that of the parlourmaid as they went down the wide stairs to the hall, where tea awaited him﻿—a very little tray on a very big, dark table.

“He’s not half a bad child,” said Susan at her tea in the servants’ quarters. “That nurse frightened him out of his little wits with her prim ways, you may depend. He’s civil enough if you speak him civil.”

“But Miss Lucy didn’t frighten him, I suppose,” said the cook; “and look how he behaved to her.”

“Well, he’s quiet enough, anyhow. You don’t hear a breath of him from morning till night,” said the upper housemaid; “seems silly-like to me.”

===Chinese===
「那个灰衣保姆说我可以拿，」菲利普说，「而且用它们搭东西又不会弄坏。我和姐姐在家一直这么玩。」他补了一句，信赖地望着客厅女仆。她夸了他搭的东西。这是他头一回在这座房子里对人提起姐姐。

「嗬，这真跟西洋景似的，」客厅女仆说；「跟我哥哥从印度寄给我的那些画片明信片一模一样。那些柱子呀、圆顶呀什么的——还有这些动物。我真不明白你怎么想得出这些东西，真不明白。」

赞美是甜的。下楼去大厅的路上，他把手滑进了客厅女仆的手里——宽宽的楼梯下去就是大厅，茶在等着他——一张很小很小的托盘，摆在一张很大很暗的桌子上。

「这孩子不坏，真的。」苏珊（Susan）在仆人厅里喝着茶说。「那个保姆规规矩矩的一套，把他吓破了小胆，你们信我的话。你客客气气跟他说话，他也客客气气的。」

「可露西小姐总没吓过他吧，」厨娘说，「瞧瞧他对人家那副做派。」

「嗯，反正他够安静的。从早到晚听不见他一点声儿。」楼上卧室的女仆说；「我觉得傻乎乎的。」

===Original===
“You slip in and look what he’s been building, that’s all,” Susan told them. “You won’t call him silly then. India an’ pagodas ain’t in it.”

They did slip in, all of them, when Philip had gone to bed. The building had progressed, though it was not finished.

“I shan’t touch a thing,” said Susan. “Let him have it to play with tomorrow. We’ll clear it all away before that nurse comes back with her caps and her collars and her stuck-up cheek.”

So next day Philip went on with his building. He put everything you can think of into it: the dominoes, and the domino-box; bricks and books; cotton-reels that he begged from Susan, and a collar-box and some cake-tins contributed by the cook. He made steps of the dominoes and a terrace of the domino-box. He got bits of southernwood out of the garden and stuck them in cotton-reels, which made beautiful pots, and they looked like bay trees in tubs. Brass finger-bowls served for domes, and the lids of brass kettles and coffeepots from the oak dresser in the hall made minarets of dazzling splendour. Chessmen were useful for minarets, too.

“I must have paved paths and a fountain,” said Philip thoughtfully. The paths were paved with mother-of-pearl card counters, and the fountain was a silver and glass ashtray, with a needlecase of filigree silver rising up from the middle of it; and the falling water was made quite nicely out of narrow bits of the silver paper off the chocolate Helen had given him at parting. Palm trees were easily made﻿—Helen had shown him how to do that﻿—with bits of larch fastened to elder stems with plasticine. There was plenty of plasticine among Lucy’s toys; there was plenty of everything.

And the city grew, till it covered the table. Philip, unwearied, set about to make another city on another table. This had for chief feature a great water-tower, with a fountain round its base; and now he stopped at nothing. He unhooked the crystal drops from the great chandeliers to make his fountains. This city was grander than the first. It had a grand tower made of a wastepaper basket and an astrologer’s tower that was a photograph-enlarging machine.

===Chinese===
「你们溜进去瞧瞧他搭了些什么，就知道了。」苏珊告诉她们。「到时候你们就不会说他傻了。印度啊、宝塔啊，跟这个一比都不算什么。」

菲利普睡下之后，她们果真一个个溜了进去。工程有了进展，虽然还没完工。

「我一样东西也不碰，」苏珊说。「让他明天接着玩。趁那个保姆还没带着她那些帽子领子、端着那副臭架子回来，我们提前收拾干净就是了。」

于是第二天菲利普接着建。他把你能想到的一切都用了进去：多米诺骨牌，还有骨牌盒；积木和书；他从苏珊那里讨来的线轴，厨娘贡献的一只领子盒和几个蛋糕模子。他用骨牌铺了台阶，用骨牌盒做了露台。他从园子里掐来几枝青蒿插在线轴里，成了漂亮的花盆，看上去就像桶栽的月桂树。黄铜洗指碗做了圆顶，大厅橡木餐具柜里黄铜水壶和咖啡壶的盖子做出了金光闪闪的尖塔。棋子用来当尖塔也很合适。

「我得有铺石的路径和一座喷泉。」菲利普若有所思地说。路径用珍珠母的纸牌筹码铺成，喷泉是一只银的和玻璃的烟灰缸，正中立起一只银丝细工的针盒；那落水则用海伦临别时给他的巧克力糖上剥下来的银纸剪成细条，做得相当好看。棕榈树好做——海伦教过他怎么做——把落叶松的小枝用橡皮泥粘在接骨木枝干上。露西的玩具里有的是橡皮泥；什么都有的是。

城就这么长大了，直到铺满整张桌子。菲利普毫无倦意，又着手在另一张桌子上建另一座城。这座城的主景是一座巨大的水塔，塔脚围着一圈喷泉；这下他什么都不管不顾了。他拧下大吊灯上的水晶坠子来做他的喷泉。这座城比第一座更宏伟。它有一座用废纸篓做的宏伟高塔，还有一座占星家的塔——那是一架照片放大机。

===Original===
The cities were really very beautiful. I wish I could describe them thoroughly to you. But it would take pages and pages. Besides all the things I have told of alone there were towers and turrets and grand staircases, pagodas and pavilions, canals made bright and water-like by strips of silver paper, and a lake with a boat on it. Philip put into his buildings all the things out of the doll’s house that seemed suitable. The wooden things-to-eat and dishes. The leaden teacups and goblets. He peopled the place with dominoes and pawns. The handsome chessmen were used for minarets. He made forts and garrisoned them with lead soldiers.

He worked hard and he worked cleverly, and as the cities grew in beauty and interestingness he loved them more and more. He was happy now. There was no time to be unhappy in.

“I will keep it as it is till Helen comes. How she will *love* it!” he said.

The two cities were connected by a bridge which was a yardstick he had found in the servants’ sewing-room and taken without hindrance, for by this time all the servants were his friends. Susan had been the first﻿—that was all.

He had just laid his bridge in place, and put Mr. and Mrs. Noah in the chief square to represent the inhabitants, and was standing rapt in admiration of his work, when a hard hand on each of his shoulders made him start and scream.

===Chinese===
这两座城实在非常美。我真想把它原原本本地描述给你听。可那得花上好多好多页。除了我已经说过的那些，还有塔楼、角楼和气派的大台阶，宝塔和亭子，一条条银纸映得波光粼粼的运河，还有一个漂着小船的湖。菲利普把玩具小屋里看着合适的摆设都搬了进去。木头的食物和碗碟。铅制的小茶杯和高脚杯。他用多米诺骨牌和小卒子往城里添了人口。气派的棋子拿来做宣礼塔。他还修了堡垒，派铅兵驻防。

他干得起劲，干得聪明，随着两座城越长越美、越来越有趣，他也越来越爱它们。他现在快乐了。根本没工夫不快乐。

「我要让它原样保持到海伦来。她会*爱*死它的！」他说。

两座城之间连着一座桥，那是他从仆人缝纫室里找到的一把码尺，拿的时候无人阻拦——因为到这时，所有的仆人都已是他的朋友了。苏珊是头一个——如此而已。

他刚把桥安放到位，又把诺亚先生和诺亚太太摆在主广场上代表城中居民，正站在那里出神地欣赏自己的作品，忽然两只硬邦邦的手抓住他的两边肩膀，吓得他一激灵，尖叫起来。

===Original===
It was the nurse. She had come back a day sooner than anyone expected her. The brother had brought home a wife, and she and the nurse had not liked each other; so she was very cross, and she took Philip by the shoulders and shook him, a thing which had never happened to him before.

“You naughty, wicked boy!” she said, still shaking.

“But I haven’t hurt anything﻿—I’ll put everything back,” he said, trembling and very pale.

“You’ll not touch any of it again,” said the nurse. “I’ll see to that. I shall put everything away myself in the morning. Taking what doesn’t belong to you!”

“But you said I might take anything I liked,” said Philip, “so if it’s wrong it’s your fault.”

“You untruthful child!” cried the nurse, and hit him over the knuckles. Now, no one had ever hit Philip before. He grew paler than ever, but he did not cry, though his hands hurt rather badly. For she had snatched up the yardstick to hit him with, and it was hard and cornery.

===Chinese===
是保姆。她比所有人预计的早回来了一天。那位兄弟领回家一个妻子，她和保姆彼此看不顺眼；所以她火气很大，一把抓住菲利普的肩膀摇晃起来——这是他从未经历过的事。

「你这个淘气、恶劣的孩子！」她说，还在摇。

「可我什么也没弄坏——我会把一切放回原处的。」他说，浑身发抖，脸色煞白。

「你一样也别想再碰了。」保姆说。「这我来办。明天早晨我会亲自把一切收走。拿不属于自己的东西！」

「可你说过我喜欢什么就可以拿什么，」菲利普说，「所以就算这不对，也是你的错。」

「你这撒谎的孩子！」保姆喊道，打了他的手指关节。要知道，从来没有人打过菲利普。他脸色比先前更白了，但他没有哭，虽然手疼得相当厉害。因为她随手抓起那把码尺来打他，那东西又硬又有棱角。

===Original===
“You are a coward,” said Philip, “and it is you who are untruthful and not me.”

“Hold your tongue,” said the nurse, and whirled him off to bed.

“You’ll get no supper, so there!” she said, angrily tucking him up.

“I don’t want any,” said Philip, “and I have to forgive you before the sun goes down.”

“Forgive, indeed!” said she, flouncing out.

“When you get sorry you’ll know I’ve forgiven you,” Philip called after her, which, of course, made her angrier than ever.

===Chinese===
「你是个懦夫，」菲利普说，「说谎的是你，不是我。」

「住嘴。」保姆说着，一把把他拽上床去。

「不给你晚饭吃，就这样！」她一边气冲冲地给他掖被子一边说。

「我不要吃，」菲利普说，「而且我得在太阳落山之前原谅你。」

「原谅，亏你说得出口！」她说着，气鼓鼓地冲了出去。

「等你后悔了，你就知道我已经原谅你了。」菲利普在她身后喊道。这话当然让她比先前更火冒三丈。

===Original===
Whether Philip cried when he was alone is not our business. Susan, who had watched the shaking and the hitting without daring to interfere, crept up later with milk and sponge-cakes. She found him asleep, and she says his eyelashes were wet.

When he awoke he thought at first that it was morning, the room was so light. But presently he saw that it was not yellow sunlight but white moonshine which made the beautiful brightness.

He wondered at first why he felt so unhappy, then he remembered how Helen had gone away and how hateful the nurse had been. And now she would pull down the city and Helen would never see it. And he would never be able to build such a beautiful one again. In the morning it would be gone, and he would not be able even to remember how it was built.

The moonlight was very bright.

“I wonder how my city looks by moonlight?” he said.

And then, all in a thrilling instant, he made up his mind to go down and see for himself how it did look.

===Chinese===
菲利普独自一人时哭没哭，不关我们的事。苏珊看着那场摇晃和抽打，不敢出面干涉，过后蹑手蹑脚端着牛奶和海绵蛋糕上了楼。她发现他已经睡着，她说他的睫毛是湿的。

他醒来时起初以为是早晨，屋里那么亮。可很快他就看清了，制造出这片美丽亮光的不是金黄的日光，而是雪白的月光。

他先是不明白自己为什么这么难过，然后想起海伦走了，保姆又那么可恶。如今她要拆掉这座城了，海伦永远见不到它了。他也永远再搭不出这么美的一座了。到早晨它就没了，他连它当初是怎么搭的都会记不清。

月光很亮。

「不知道我的城在月光下是什么样子？」他说。

接着，在一个令人战栗的瞬间，他打定了主意，要亲自下去看看它到底什么样。

===Original===
He slipped on his dressing-gown, opened his door softly, and crept along the corridor and down the broad staircase, then along the gallery and into the drawing-room. It was very dark, but he felt his way to a window and undid the shutter, and there lay his city, flooded with moonlight, just as he had imagined it.

He gazed on it for a moment in ecstasy and then turned to shut the door. As he did so he felt a slight strange giddiness and stood a moment with his hand to his head. He turned and went again towards the city, and when he was close to it he gave a little cry, hastily stifled, for fear someone should hear him and come down and send him to bed. He stood and gazed about him bewildered and, once more, rather giddy. For the city had, in a quick blink of light, followed by darkness, disappeared. So had the drawing-room. So had the chair that stood close to the table. He could see mountainous shapes raising enormous heights in the distance, and the moonlight shone on the tops of them. But he himself seemed to be in a vast, flat plain. There was the softness of long grass round his feet, but there were no trees, no houses, no hedges or fences to break the expanse of grass. It seemed darker in some parts than others. That was all. It reminded him of the illimitable prairie of which he had read in books of adventure.

“I suppose I’m dreaming,” said Philip, “though I don’t see how I can have gone to sleep just while I was turning the door handle. However﻿—”

He stood still expecting that something would happen. In dreams something always does happen, if it’s only that the dream comes to an end. But nothing happened now﻿—Philip just stood there quite quietly and felt the warm soft grass round his ankles.

Then, as his eyes became used to the darkness of the plain, he saw some way off a very steep bridge leading up to a dark height on whose summit the moon shone whitely. He walked towards it, and as he approached he saw that it was less like a bridge than a sort of ladder, and that it rose to a giddy height above him. It seemed to rest on a rock far up against dark sky, and the inside of the rock seemed hollowed out in one vast dark cave.

And now he was close to the foot of the ladder. It had no rungs, but narrow ledges made hold for feet and hands. Philip remembered Jack and the Beanstalk, and looked up longingly; but the ladder was a very very long one. On the other hand, it was the only thing that seemed to lead anywhere, and he had had enough of standing lonely in the grassy prairie, where he seemed to have been for a very long time indeed. So he put his hands and feet to the ladder and began to go up. It was a very long climb. There were three hundred and eight steps, for he counted them. And the steps were only on one side of the ladder, so he had to be extremely careful. On he went, up and on, on and up, till his feet ached and his hands felt as though they would drop off for tiredness. He could not look up far, and he dared not look down at all. There was nothing for it but to climb and climb and climb, and at last he saw the ground on which the ladder rested﻿—a terrace hewn in regular lines, and, as it seemed, hewn from the solid rock. His head was level with the ground, now his hands, now his feet. He leaped sideways from the ladder and threw himself face down on the ground, which was cold and smooth like marble. There he lay, drawing deep breaths of weariness and relief.

===Chinese===
他披上晨衣，轻轻开了门，沿着走廊溜过去，下了宽宽的大楼梯，再沿着画廊走进客厅。屋里很黑，可他摸到一扇窗，打开了窗板，他的城就躺在那里，浸在月光里，正如他想象的那样。

他出神地凝望了它片刻，然后转身去关门。就在这时，他感到一阵轻微而奇怪的眩晕，伸手扶着头站了一会儿。他转身又朝那座城走去，走到近处时他低呼了一声，又赶紧憋住，生怕有人听见、下来把他打发回床上去。他站在那里，茫然地环顾四周，又一次感到眩晕。因为那座城，在一阵急促的光亮、随之而来的黑暗之后，消失了。客厅也消失了。桌子旁边立着的那把椅子也消失了。他看得见远处有山一样的形状隆起到惊人的高度，月光照在那些山顶上。可他自己似乎站在一片广袤平坦的平原上。脚边是长草的柔软，却没有树，没有房子，没有树篱或栅栏来打断这片草的旷野。有些地方比别处更暗。就是这样。这让他想起他在冒险书里读到过的一望无际的大草原。

「我看我是在做梦，」菲利普说，「虽然我不明白，我怎么会在刚要去转门把手的时候就睡着了。不过——」

他站着不动，等着有什么事情发生。在梦里总有事情发生，哪怕只是梦醒了。可现在什么也没发生——菲利普只是安安静静地站在那里，感觉着脚踝边温软的青草。

后来，等他的眼睛适应了平原上的黑暗，他看见不远处有一座非常陡的桥，通向一处黑暗的高地，月光正白白地照在高地的顶上。他朝它走去，走近了才发现，它不像一座桥，倒更像一架梯子，高耸在他头顶，高得让人发晕。它似乎架在远处顶着黑暗天空的岩石上，岩石的内部似乎掏空了，成了一个巨大的黑洞洞的洞穴。

现在他到了梯子脚下。它没有横档，只有窄窄的檐状蹬板供手脚攀抓。菲利普想起了《杰克与豆茎》，渴望地抬头望去；可这梯子实在太长太长了。但另一方面，它是唯一看上去通向什么地方的东西，而他也实在受够了孤零零地站在青草平原上——他好像已经在这儿站了很久很久了。于是他手脚并用，开始往上爬。那是很长很长的一段攀爬。共有 308 级，因为他数了。蹬板只在梯子的一面，所以他得万分小心。他爬呀爬，向上再向前，向前再向上，直到脚都疼了，手累得好像要掉下来。他往上看不了太远，更根本不敢往下看。没有别的办法，只有爬呀爬呀爬，最后他终于看见了梯子所架的那片地面——一块按整齐的线条凿出来的平台，而且似乎是从整块岩石上凿出来的。他的头先和地面齐平，然后是手，然后是脚。他侧身从梯子上跃下，趴倒在地上，那地面像大理石一样又冷又光滑。他趴在那里，疲惫而宽慰地深深喘着气。

===Original===
There was a great silence all about, which rested and soothed, and presently he rose and looked around him. He was close to an archway with very thick pillars, and he went towards it and peeped cautiously in. It seemed to be a great gate leading to an open space, and beyond it he could see dim piles that looked like churches and houses. But all was deserted; the moonlight and he had the place, whatever it was, to themselves.

“I suppose everyone’s in bed,” said Philip, and stood there trembling a little, but very curious and interested, in the black shadow of the strange arch.

===Chinese===
四下一片巨大的寂静，让人安宁、使人平静，过了一会儿，他站起来环顾四周。他离一道柱子极粗的拱门很近，他朝它走过去，小心翼翼地朝里张望。那似乎是一道通向一片开阔地的大门，门外隐约可见一堆堆像是教堂和房子的东西。可一切都荒无人烟；月光和他独占了这地方，不管它是什么地方。

「我看大家都睡了。」菲利普说。他在那道陌生拱门的黑色阴影里站着，微微发抖，却非常好奇、非常有兴致。

## II Deliverer or Destroyer / 解救者还是毁灭者

===Original===
Philip stood in the shadow of the dark arch and looked out. He saw before him a great square surrounded by tall irregular buildings. In the middle was a fountain whose waters, silver in the moonlight, rose and fell with gentle plashing sound. A tall tree, close to the archway, cast the shadow of its trunk across the path﻿—a broad black bar. He listened, listened, listened, but there was nothing to listen to, except the deep night silence and the changing soft sound the fountain made.

His eyes, growing accustomed to the dimness, showed him that he was under a heavy domed roof supported on large square pillars﻿—to the right and left stood dark doors, shut fast.

“I will explore these doors by daylight,” he said. He did not feel exactly frightened. But he did not feel exactly brave either. But he wished and intended to be brave, so he said, “I will explore these doors. At least I think I will,” he added, for one must not only be brave but truthful.

And then suddenly he felt very sleepy. He leaned against the wall, and presently it seemed that sitting down would be less trouble, and then that lying down would be more truly comfortable. A bell from very very far away sounded the hour, twelve. Philip counted up to nine, but he missed the tenth bell-beat, and the eleventh and the twelfth as well, because he was fast asleep cuddled up warmly in the thick quilted dressing-gown that Helen had made him last winter. He dreamed that everything was as it used to be before That Man came and changed everything and took Helen away. He was in his own little bed in his own little room in their own little house, and Helen had come to call him. He could see the sunlight through his closed eyelids﻿—he was keeping them closed just for the fun of hearing her try to wake him, and presently he would tell her he had been awake all the time, and they would laugh together about it. And then he awoke, and he was not in his soft bed at home but on the hard floor of a big, strange gatehouse, and it was not Helen who was shaking him and saying, “Here﻿—I say, wake up, can’t you,” but a tall man in a red coat; and the light that dazzled his eyes was not from the sun at all, but from a horn lantern which the man was holding close to his face.

===Chinese===
菲利普站在黑暗拱门的阴影里，向外望去。他看见面前是一个大广场，四周环绕着高高低低、错落不齐的建筑。广场正中是一座喷泉，泉水在月光下泛着银光，起起落落，发出轻柔的哗哗声。紧挨着拱门有一株高树，树干的影子横过小径——一道宽宽的黑杠。他听了又听，听了又听，可什么也没有，只有深夜的寂静，和喷泉变幻的轻柔水声。

他的眼睛渐渐习惯了昏暗，看出自己正置身于一座沉重的穹顶之下，穹顶架在巨大的方形柱子上——左右两边都是黑黑的门，关得严严实实。

「我要等天亮了再探这些门。」他说。他谈不上真的害怕，可也谈不上真的勇敢。不过他盼着自己勇敢，也打定了主意要勇敢，于是他说：「我要探一探这些门。至少我想我会的。」他又补了一句，因为人不但要勇敢，还要诚实。

接着，他忽然觉得非常困。他靠着墙，不一会儿觉得坐下省力些，再一会儿又觉得躺下才真正舒服。一座钟在极远极远的地方敲响了时辰，十二下。菲利普数到第九下，就漏了第十下，也漏了第十一下和第十二下，因为他已经睡熟了，暖暖和和地蜷在厚厚的绗缝晨衣里——那是海伦去年冬天给他做的。他梦见一切还是老样子，在「那个男人」来了、改变一切、把海伦带走之前的老样子。他躺在自己小房间里的小床上，在他们自己的小房子里，海伦来叫他了。他闭着眼也看得见阳光——他故意闭着，就为了享受听她想办法叫醒他的乐趣，待会儿他要告诉她，自己其实一直醒着，然后两个人一起大笑一场。接着他醒了，他不是躺在家里的软床上，而是躺在又大又陌生的门房里坚硬的地板上，摇着他、说着「喂——我说，你醒醒，行不行」的不是海伦，而是一个穿红衣裳的高个子男人；晃得他睁不开眼的光根本不是日光，而是那人举到他脸旁的一盏牛角灯笼的光。

===Original===
“What’s the matter?” said Philip sleepily.

“That’s the question,” said the man in red. “Come along to the guardroom and give an account of yourself, you young shaver.”

He took Philip’s ear gently but firmly between a very hard finger and thumb.

“Leave go,” said Philip, “I’m not going to run away.” And he stood up feeling very brave.

The man shifted his hold from ear to shoulder and led Philip through one of those doors which he had thought of exploring by daylight. It was not daylight yet, and the room, large and bare, with an arch at each end and narrow little windows at the sides, was lighted by horn lanterns and tall tapers in pewter candlesticks. It seemed to Philip that the room was full of soldiers.

Their captain, with a good deal of gold about him and a very smart black moustache, got up from a bench.

===Chinese===
「怎么回事？」菲利普睡意惺忪地说。

「这正是问题所在。」红衣男人说。「跟我到卫兵室去交代你的来历，小毛孩。」

他用非常坚硬的拇指和食指，温和却不容分说地夹住了菲利普的耳朵。

「放开，」菲利普说，「我又不跑。」他站起身来，觉得自己非常勇敢。

那人把擒拿的地方从耳朵换到肩膀，领着菲利普穿过了那几扇门中的一扇——他原本想等天亮再来探的。天还没有亮，这间屋子又大又空，两头各有一个拱门，侧面开着窄窄的小窗，屋里点着牛角灯笼和插在白蜡烛台上的高蜡烛。菲利普只觉得满屋子都是士兵。

他们的卫队长（the captain）从长凳上站起身来，他一身金饰，留着一把神气的黑胡子。

===Original===
“Look what I’ve caught, sir,” said the man who owned the hand on Philip’s shoulder.

“Humph,” said the captain, “so it’s really happened at last.”

“What has?” said Philip.

“Why, you have,” said the captain. “Don’t be frightened, little man.”

“I’m not frightened,” said Philip, and added politely, “I should be so much obliged if you’d tell me what you mean.” He added something which he had heard people say when they asked the way to the market or the public gardens, “I’m quite a stranger here,” he said.

A jolly roar of laughter went up from the redcoats.

===Chinese===
「瞧我抓到了什么，长官。」把手按在菲利普肩上的那人说。

「哼，」卫队长说，「这么说，它到底还是发生了。」

「什么发生了？」菲利普说。

「就是你呀。」卫队长说。「别害怕，小家伙。」

「我才不害怕。」菲利普说，又礼貌地补了一句：「要是您肯告诉我您是什么意思，我将感激不尽。」他还添了一句自己听人家问去市场或公共花园的路时说的话。「我在这里完全是人生地不熟。」他说。

红衣士兵们爆发出一阵快活的大笑。

===Original===
“It isn’t manners to laugh at strangers,” said Philip.

“Mind your own manners,” said the captain sharply; “in this country little boys speak when they’re spoken to. Stranger, eh? Well, we knew that, you know!”

Philip, though he felt snubbed, yet felt grand too. Here he was in the middle of an adventure with grown-up soldiers. He threw out his chest and tried to look manly.

The captain sat down in a chair at the end of a long table, drew a black book to him﻿—a black book covered with dust﻿—and began to rub a rusty pen-nib on his sword, which was not rusty.

“Come now,” he said, opening the book, “tell me how you came here. And mind you speak the truth.”

“I *always* speak the truth,” said Philip proudly.

===Chinese===
「笑话陌生人是不礼貌的。」菲利普说。

「先管好你自己的礼貌吧。」卫队长厉声说；「在这个国家，小孩子要等大人问了话才开口。陌生人，嗯？哼，这个我们早就知道啦！」

菲利普虽然觉得受了抢白，却也觉得威风。瞧瞧他，正置身在一场和大兵们同处的冒险当中。他挺起胸膛，尽力显得像个男子汉。

卫队长在长桌尽头的一把椅子上坐下，把一本黑色的书拉到面前——一本积满灰尘的黑皮书——开始在一把没生锈的剑上，蹭一支生了锈的笔尖。

「好了，」他翻开书，说，「告诉我你是怎么来的。记住要说实话。」

「我*一向*说实话。」菲利普骄傲地说。

===Original===
All the soldiers rose and saluted him with looks of deep surprise and respect.

“Well, nearly always,” said Philip, hot to the ears, and the soldiers clattered stiffly down again on to the benches, laughing once more. Philip had imagined there to be more discipline in the army.

“How did you come here?” said the captain.

“Up the great bridge staircase,” said Philip.

The captain wrote busily in the book.

“What did you come for?”

===Chinese===
全体士兵起立，向他敬礼，一脸深深的惊讶和敬意。

「呃，差不多一向。」菲利普说，耳朵根都热了，士兵们咔啦咔啦地重新坐回长凳上，又笑起来。菲利普原以为军队里的纪律要严明得多呢。

「你是怎么来的？」卫队长说。

「从那道大桥梯爬上来的。」菲利普说。

卫队长在本子上飞快地记。

「你来干什么？」

===Original===
“I didn’t know what else to do. There was nothing but illimitable prairie﻿—and so I came up.”

“You are a very bold boy,” said the captain.

“Thank you,” said Philip. “I do *want* to be.”

“What was your purpose in coming?”

“I didn’t do it on purpose﻿—I just happened to come.”

The captain wrote that down too. And then he and Philip and the soldiers looked at each other in silence.

===Chinese===
「我不知道还能干什么。四下里只有一望无际的大草原——所以我就上来了。」

「你是个非常大胆的孩子。」卫队长说。

「谢谢。」菲利普说。「我*就是想*当这样的人。」

「你来的目的何在？」

「我不是故意的——我只是碰巧来了。」

卫队长把这个也记了下来。然后，他、菲利普和士兵们面面相觑，一片沉默。

===Original===
“Well?” said the boy.

“Well?” said the captain.

“I do wish,” said the boy, “you’d tell me what you meant by my really happening after all. And then I wish you’d tell me the way home.”

“Where do you want to get to?” asked the captain.

“The *address*,” said Philip, “is The Grange, Ravelsham, Sussex.”

“Don’t know it,” said the captain briefly, “and anyhow you can’t go back there now. Didn’t you read the notice at the top of the ladder? Trespassers will be prosecuted. You’ve got to be prosecuted before you can go back anywhere.”

===Chinese===
「怎么样？」男孩说。

「怎么样？」卫队长说。

「我真希望，」男孩说，「您肯告诉我，您说『到底还是发生了』是什么意思。然后还希望您告诉我回家的路。」

「你要回哪儿？」卫队长问。

「*地址*嘛，」菲利普说，「是萨塞克斯郡拉维尔沙姆（Ravelsham）的格兰奇庄园。」

「不知道，」卫队长简短地说，「反正你现在哪儿也回不去了。你没看见梯子顶上贴的告示吗？闲人免进，违者法办。你得先法办了，才能回任何地方去。」

===Original===
“I’d rather be persecuted than go down that ladder again,” he said. “I suppose it won’t be very bad﻿—being persecuted, I mean?”

His idea of persecution was derived from books. He thought it to be something vaguely unpleasant from which one escaped in disguise﻿—adventurous and always successful.

“That’s for the judges to decide,” said the captain, “it’s a serious thing trespassing in our city. This guard is put here expressly to prevent it.”

“Do you have many trespassers?” Philip asked. The captain seemed kind, and Philip had a great-uncle who was a judge, so the word judges made him think of tips and good advice, rather than of justice and punishment.

“Many trespassers indeed!” the captain almost snorted his answer. “That’s just it. There’s never been one before. You’re the first. For years and years and years there’s been a guard here, because when the town was first built the astrologers foretold that some day there would be a trespasser who would do untold mischief. So it’s our privilege﻿—we’re the Polistopolitan guards﻿—to keep watch over the only way by which a trespasser could come in.”

“May I sit down?” said Philip suddenly, and the soldiers made room for him on the bench.

===Chinese===
「我宁可被法办，也不肯再下去爬那道梯子。」他说。「我想，被法办不会太难受吧——我是说法办这件事？」

他对受迫害的想象全是从书本里来的。他想那是某种模模糊糊不痛快的事，化个装就能逃掉——又冒险，又回回成功。

「那要由法官们决定。」卫队长说，「擅闯我们的城，可是件严重的事。这支卫队就是专门派来防这个的。」

「你们有很多擅闯的人吗？」菲利普问。卫队长似乎挺和气，而菲利普有一位当法官的伯祖父，所以「法官」这个词让他想到的是小费和善意的忠告，而不是公道和惩罚。

「多得很哪！」卫队长几乎是哼着答出来的。「正是这么回事。以前一个也没有过。你是头一个。年年岁岁，这支卫队一直驻在这儿，因为建城之初，占星家们预言过，有朝一日会来一个擅闯者，闯下说不尽的祸事。所以这是我们的殊荣——我们是波利斯托波利斯卫队（the Polistopolitan guards）——守望着擅闯者有可能进来的唯一一条路。」

「我可以坐下吗？」菲利普忽然说，士兵们在长凳上给他让出了位子。

===Original===
“My father and my grandfather and all my ancestors were in the guards,” said the captain proudly. “It’s a very great honour.”

“I wonder,” said Philip, “why you don’t cut off the end of your ladder﻿—the top end I mean; then nobody could come up.”

“That would never do,” said the captain, “because, you see, there’s another prophecy. The great deliverer is to come that way.”

“Couldn’t I,” suggested Philip shyly, “couldn’t I be the deliverer instead of the trespasser? I’d much rather, you know.”

“I daresay you would,” said the captain; “but people can’t be deliverers just because they’d much rather, you know.”

“And isn’t anyone to come up the ladder bridge except just those two?”

===Chinese===
「我父亲、我祖父、我所有的祖先，都在卫队里当过差。」卫队长骄傲地说。「这是天大的荣耀。」

「我不明白，」菲利普说，「你们干吗不把梯子砍掉一截——我是说上头那一截；那就谁也上不来了。」

「那可万万使不得，」卫队长说，「因为，你瞧，还有另一条预言呢。那位伟大的解救者（the Deliverer）将从那条路到来。」

「那我不行吗，」菲利普怯生生地提议，「我不能当解救者，别当擅闯者吗？您知道，那样我情愿得多。」

「我敢说你情愿，」卫队长说；「可人不能光凭自己情愿，就当上解救者，你懂吗。」

「那么除了那两位，就没有人能上梯桥来了吗？」

===Original===
“We don’t know; that’s just it. You know what prophecies are.”

“I’m afraid I don’t﻿—exactly.”

“So vague and mixed up, I mean. The one I’m telling you about goes something like this.

===Chinese===
「我们不知道；正是这么回事。你知道预言是什么玩意儿。」

「恐怕我不太——不大知道。」

「我是说，又含糊，又搅缠不清。我跟你说起的那一条，大概是这么个样子。
===Original===
> “Who comes up the ladder stair?
 

 Beware, beware,
 

 Steely eyes and copper hair
 

 Strife and grief and pain to bear
 

 All come up the ladder stair.”

===Chinese===
> 「谁人登上梯桥来？

 



 当心，当心，

 



 钢般的眼睛，铜般的头发，

 



 纷争、忧伤、苦痛要捱，

 



 全都登上梯桥来。」

===Original===
“You see we can’t tell whether that means one person or a lot of people with steely eyes and copper hair.”

“My hair’s just plain boy-colour,” said Philip; “my sister says so, and my eyes are blue, I believe.”

“I can’t see in this light;” the captain leaned his elbows on the table and looked earnestly in the boy’s eyes. “No, I can’t see. The other prophecy goes:

===Chinese===
「你瞧，我们说不清，这是指一个人，还是一大帮钢眼睛铜头发的人。」

「我的头发就是普普通通的男孩色，」菲利普说；「我姐姐这么说的。还有，我相信我的眼睛是蓝的。」

「这光线下我看不出来；」卫队长把胳膊肘撑在桌上，认真地盯着男孩的眼睛看。「不，我看不出来。另一条预言是这么说的：

===Original===
> “From down and down and very far down
 

 The king shall come to take his own;
 

 He shall deliver the Magic town,
 

 And all that he made shall be his own.
 

 Beware, take care. Beware, prepare,
 

 The king shall come by the ladder stair.”

===Chinese===
> 「自深深之下，迢迢远远，

 



 君王将临，取回他自有的城邦；

 



 他将解救这魔法之城，

 



 凡他手造的，尽归他名下。

 



 当心，提防。当心，整装，

 



 君王将由梯桥还乡。」

===Original===
“How jolly,” said Philip; “I love poetry. Do you know any more?”

“There are heaps of prophecies of course,” said the captain; “the astrologers must do *something* to earn their pay. There’s rather a nice one:

===Chinese===
「真带劲，」菲利普说；「我爱诗歌。您还知道别的吗？」

「预言当然有一大堆，」卫队长说；「占星家们总得*干点什么*，才对得起他们的俸禄。有一条还挺不错的：

===Original===
> Every night when the bright stars blink
 

 The guards shall turn out, and have a drink
 

 As the clock strikes two.
 

 And every night when no stars are seen
 

 The guards shall drink in their own canteen
 

 When the clock strikes two.

===Chinese===
> 每夜亮星眨眼的时候，

 



 卫队要出勤，喝上一口，

 



 当时钟敲两点。

 



 每夜一颗星也看不见，

 



 卫队要在自家食堂里喝个酣，

 



 当时钟敲两点。

===Original===
“Tonight there aren’t any stars, so we have the drinks served here. It’s less trouble than going across the square to the canteen, and the principle’s the same. Principle is the great thing with a prophecy, my boy.”

“Yes,” said Philip. And then the faraway bell beat again. One, two. And outside was a light patter of feet.

A soldier rose﻿—saluted his officer and threw open the door. There was a moment’s pause; Philip expected someone to come in with a tray and glasses, as they did at his great-uncle’s when gentlemen were suddenly thirsty at times that were not mealtimes.

But instead, after a moment’s pause, a dozen greyhounds stepped daintily in on their padded catlike feet; and round the neck of each dog was slung a roundish thing that looked like one of the little barrels which St. Bernard dogs wear round their necks in the pictures. And when these were loosened and laid on the table Philip was charmed to see that the roundish things were not barrels but coconuts.

The soldiers reached down some pewter pots from a high shelf﻿—pierced the coconuts with their bayonets and poured out the coconut milk. They all had drinks, so the prophecy came true, and what is more they gave Philip a drink as well. It was delicious, and there was as much of it as he wanted. I have never had as much coconut milk as I wanted. Have you?

Then the hollow coconuts were tied on to the dogs’ necks again and out they went, slim and beautiful, two by two, wagging their slender tails, in the most amiable and orderly way.

===Chinese===
「今晚一颗星也没有，所以我们让人把酒水送到这儿来。比穿过广场去食堂省事，道理是一样的。对预言来说，要紧的是道理，我的孩子。」

「嗯。」菲利普说。这时，那遥远的钟又敲了起来。一下，两下。门外传来轻轻的、嗒嗒的脚步声。

一个士兵站起身——向军官敬了个礼，把门推开。停了片刻；菲利普还以为会有谁端着托盘和杯子进来，就像在他伯祖父家，先生们在不是吃饭的时辰突然口渴时那样。

可不是。片刻停顿之后，十几条灵缇犬迈着带肉垫的猫一般的脚，轻盈优雅地走了进来；每条狗的脖子上都挂着一个圆圆的东西，看着就像画里圣伯纳犬脖子上挂的那种小桶。等这些物件解下来摆到桌上，菲利普高兴地看到，那些圆圆的东西不是小桶，而是椰子。

士兵们从高架子上取下几只白蜡壶——用刺刀凿开椰子，倒出椰子汁。人人有份，预言应验了，而且他们还给了菲利普一份。好喝极了，想要多少有多少。我就从来没有过想喝多少就有多少的椰子汁。你呢？

随后，空椰子壳又绑回狗脖子上，它们出去了，苗条又漂亮，两条两条地，摇着细尾巴，和和气气，规规矩矩。

===Original===
“They take the coconuts to the town kitchen,” said the captain, “to be made into coconut ice for the army breakfast; waste not want not, you know. We don’t waste anything here, my boy.” Philip had quite got over his snubbing. He now felt that the captain was talking with him as man to man. Helen had gone away and left him; well, he was learning to do without Helen. And he had got away from the Grange, and Lucy, and that nurse. He was a man among men. And then, just as he was feeling most manly and important, and quite equal to facing any number of judges, there came a little tap at the door of the guardroom, and a very little voice said:

“Oh, do please let me come in.”

Then the door opened slowly.

“Well, come in, whoever you are,” said the captain. And the person who came in was﻿—Lucy. Lucy, whom Philip thought he had got rid of﻿—Lucy, who stood for the new hateful life to which Helen had left him. Lucy, in her serge skirt and jersey, with her little sleek fair pigtails, and that anxious “I-wish-we-could-be-friends’ smile of hers. Philip was furious. It was too bad.

“And who is this?” the captain was saying kindly.

“It’s me﻿—it’s Lucy,” she said. “I came up with *him*.”

===Chinese===
「它们把椰子送到城里的大厨房去，」卫队长说，「做成军队早餐吃的椰子冰；你知道的，不糟蹋就不愁缺。我们这儿什么都不糟蹋，我的孩子。」菲利普早已把受抢白的事丢开了。他现在觉得，卫队长是在跟他男人对男人地说话。海伦走了，丢下了他；好啊，他正在学着没有海伦也过得下去。他也逃离了格兰奇庄园，逃离了露西，逃离了那个保姆。他是男人堆里的男人了。可就在他觉得自己最男子汉、最了不起，多少个法官都顶得住的时候，卫兵室的门上响起轻轻的敲门声，一个很轻很轻的声音说：

「哦，求求你们让我进来吧。」

然后，门慢慢开了。

「行啊，进来吧，不管你是谁。」卫队长说。进来的人是——露西。菲利普以为已经甩掉了的露西——代表着海伦丢给他的那种可恨的新生活的露西。穿着粗呢裙和针织上衣的露西，梳着两条光溜溜的金色小辫子，脸上挂着她那种「真希望我们能做朋友」的赔小心笑容。菲利普火冒三丈。太气人了。

「这位是谁呀？」卫队长正和气地问。

「是我——我是露西。」她说。「我是跟着*他*来的。」

===Original===
She pointed to Philip. “No manners,” thought Philip in bitterness.

“No, you didn’t,” he said shortly.

“I did﻿—I was close behind you when you were climbing the ladder bridge. And I’ve been waiting alone ever since, when you were asleep and all. I *knew* he’d be cross when he knew I’d come,” she explained to the soldiers.

“I’m *not* cross,” said Philip very crossly indeed, but the captain signed to him to be silent. Then Lucy was questioned and her answers written in the book, and when that was done the captain said:

“So this little girl is a friend of yours?”

“No, she isn’t,” said Philip violently; “she’s not my friend, and she never will be. I’ve seen her, that’s all, and I don’t want to see her again.”

===Chinese===
她指了指菲利普。「没规矩。」菲利普恨恨地想。

「不，你没有。」他硬邦邦地说。

「我有——你爬梯桥的时候，我就紧紧跟在你后面。后来你睡着了，干这干那的，我就一直一个人等着。我*就知道*，他知道了会生气的。」她对士兵们解释。

「我才*没有*生气。」菲利普说，口气实在气呼呼，可卫队长示意他安静。接着露西受了问话，回答记进了本子，问完了，卫队长说：

「这么说，这位小姑娘是你的朋友？」

「不，她不是。」菲利普激烈地说；「她不是我的朋友，永远也不会是。我见过她，就这么回事，我再也不想见到她了。」

===Original===
“You *are* unkind,” said Lucy.

And then there was a grave silence, most unpleasant to Philip. The soldiers, he perceived, now looked coldly at him. It was all Lucy’s fault. What did she want to come shoving in for, spoiling everything? Anyone but a girl would have known that a guardroom wasn’t the right place for a girl. He frowned and said nothing. Lucy had smuggled up against the captain’s knee, and he was stroking her hair.

“Poor little woman,” he said. “You must go to sleep now, so as to be rested before you go to the Hall of Justice in the morning.”

They made Lucy a bed of soldiers’ cloaks laid on a bench; and bearskins are the best of pillows. Philip had a soldier’s cloak and a bench, and a bearskin too﻿—but what was the good? Everything was spoiled. If Lucy had not come the guardroom as a sleeping-place would have been almost as good as the tented field. But she *had* come, and the guardroom was no better now than any old night-nursery. And how had she known? How had she come? How had she made her way to that illimitable prairie where he had found the mysterious beginning of the ladder bridge? He went to sleep a bunched-up lump of prickly discontent and suppressed fury.

===Chinese===
「你*真*狠心。」露西说。

接着是一片沉沉的寂静，菲利普觉得说不出的难堪。他察觉到，士兵们这会儿看他的眼神冷冷的。这全是露西的错。她干吗非要挤进来，把一切都搅黄？只要不是女孩子，谁都知道卫兵室不是女孩子待的地方。他皱着眉，一言不发。露西已经偎到了卫队长的膝盖边，他正抚着她的头发。

「可怜的小人儿。」他说。「你现在得睡了，好养足精神，明早去正义大厅（the Hall of Justice）。」

他们用士兵的斗篷在长凳上给露西铺了张床；熊皮帽是最好的枕头。菲利普也得了一条士兵斗篷、一张长凳，还有一顶熊皮帽——可那有什么用？一切都给搅黄了。要是露西没来，睡在卫兵室几乎不比睡营帐差。可她*偏偏*来了，卫兵室这下跟随便哪间旧夜间育儿室一样没劲了。她是怎么知道的？她是怎么来的？她是怎么一路摸到那片一望无际的大草原、找到梯桥那神秘的起点的？他睡着的时候，蜷成一小团，满心是刺蓬蓬的委屈和憋着的火气。

===Original===
When he woke it was bright daylight, and a soldier was saying, “Wake up, Trespassers. Breakfast﻿—”

“How jolly,” thought Philip, “to be having military breakfast.” Then he remembered Lucy, and hated her being there, and felt once more that she had spoiled everything.

I should not, myself, care for a breakfast of coconut ice, peppermint creams, apples, bread and butter and sweet milk. But the soldiers seemed to enjoy it. And it would have exactly suited Philip if he had not seen that Lucy was enjoying it too.

“I do hate greedy girls,” he told himself, for he was now in that state of black rage when you hate everything the person you are angry with does or says or is.

And now it was time to start for the Hall of Justice. The guard formed outside, and Philip noticed that each soldier stood on a sort of green mat. When the order to march was given, each soldier quickly and expertly rolled up his green mat and put it under his arm. And whenever they stopped, because of the crowd, each soldier unrolled his green mat, and stood on it till it was time to go on again. And they had to stop several times, for the crowd was very thick in the great squares and in the narrow streets of the city. It was a wonderful crowd. There were men and women and children in every sort of dress. Italian, Spanish, Russian; French peasants in blue blouses and wooden shoes, workmen in the dress English working people wore a hundred years ago. Norwegians, Swedes, Swiss, Turks, Greeks, Indians, Arabians, Chinese, Japanese, besides Red Indians in dresses of skins, and Scots in kilts and sporrans. Philip did not know what nation most of the dresses belonged to﻿—to him it was a brilliant patchwork of gold and gay colours. It reminded him of the fancy-dress party he had once been to with Helen, when he wore a Pierrot’s dress and felt very silly in it. He noticed that not a single boy in all that crowd was dressed as he was﻿—in what he thought was the only correct dress for boys. Lucy walked beside him. Once, just after they started, she said, “Aren’t you frightened, Philip?” and he would not answer, though he longed to say, “Of course not. It’s only girls who are afraid.” But he thought it would be more disagreeable to say nothing, so he said it.

===Chinese===
他醒来时天已大亮，一个士兵正喊着：「醒醒，擅闯犯们。早饭——」

「真带劲，」菲利普想，「吃上军营早餐了。」然后他想起了露西，恨她在场，又一次觉得她把一切都搅黄了。

拿我自己来说，椰子冰、薄荷奶油糖、苹果、黄油面包加甜牛奶这样的早餐，我是不稀罕的。可士兵们好像吃得很香。要不是菲利普看见露西也吃得香，这早餐本会正合他的心意。

「我讨厌贪吃的女孩子。」他对自己说，因为他这会儿正处在那种狂怒里——你气的那个人做的事、说的话、整个人，你样样都恨。

这时候，该动身去正义大厅了。卫队在外面列队，菲利普注意到，每个士兵都站在一块绿色的垫子上。出发令一下，每个士兵都麻利地卷起绿垫子，往胳膊底下一夹。每当因为人挤停下来，每个士兵就摊开绿垫子，站在上面，直到再开拔。他们停了好几回，因为城里的广场上、窄街上，人群密得很。那真是奇妙的人群。男女老少，什么衣裳都有。意大利的、西班牙的、俄国的；穿蓝罩衫和木鞋的法国农民，穿一百年前英国劳动人民那种衣裳的工人。挪威人、瑞典人、瑞士人、土耳其人、希腊人、印度人、阿拉伯人、中国人、日本人，还有穿兽皮衣的红种印第安人，穿褶裙、挂毛皮袋的苏格兰人。那些衣裳多半属于哪个民族，菲利普认不出来——在他眼里，那是一片金色和艳色织成的灿烂拼花。这让他想起他跟海伦去过的那次化装舞会，他扮丑角皮埃罗（Pierrot），穿着那身衣裳自觉傻透了。他还注意到，那么大一群人里，没有一个男孩穿得跟他一样——穿他所以为的男孩唯一正确的装束。露西走在他旁边。刚出发那会儿，她问过一次：「你不害怕吗，菲利普？」他不肯回答，虽然他巴不得说出口：「当然不怕。只有女孩子才害怕呢。」可他想了想，觉得一声不吭更气人，于是他就把这话说了出来。
===Original===
When they got to the Hall of Justice, she caught hold of his hand, and said:

“Oh!” very loud and sudden, “doesn’t it remind you of anything?” she asked.

Philip pulled his hand away and said “No” before he remembered that he had decided not to speak to her. And the “No” was quite untrue, for the building did remind him of something, though he couldn’t have told you what.

The prisoners and their guard passed through a great arch between magnificent silver pillars, and along a vast corridor, lined with soldiers who all saluted.

“Do all sorts of soldiers salute you?” he asked the captain, “or only just your own ones?”

“It’s *you* they’re saluting,” the captain said; “our laws tell us to salute all prisoners out of respect for their misfortunes.”

===Chinese===
到了正义大厅跟前，她一把抓住他的手，说：

「哦！」又响又突然，「这让你不想起点什么吗？」她问。

菲利普把手抽了回来，还没想起自己打定过主意不跟她说话，就说了声「不像」。这句「不像」可完全是瞎话，因为那栋建筑确实让他想起了什么，只是他说不上来是什么。

囚犯和押送的卫队穿过一道宏伟的拱门，拱门立在壮丽的银柱之间，再沿一条长长的走廊走去，走廊两旁列队的士兵全体敬礼。

「什么兵都向你们敬礼吗？」他问卫队长，「还是只有您自己的兵？」

「他们敬的是*你*。」卫队长说；「我们的法律吩咐我们，出于对不幸者的敬意，要向一切囚犯敬礼。」

===Original===
The judge sat on a high bronze throne with colossal bronze dragons on each side of it, and wide shallow steps of ivory, black and white.

Two attendants spread a round mat on the top of the steps in front of the judge﻿—a yellow mat it was, and very thick, and he stood up and saluted the prisoners. (“Because of your misfortunes,” the captain whispered.)

The judge wore a bright yellow robe with a green girdle, and he had no wig, but a very odd-shaped hat, which he kept on all the time.

The trial did not last long, and the captain said very little, and the judge still less, while the prisoners were not allowed to speak at all. The judge looked up something in a book, and consulted in a low voice with the crown lawyer and a sour-faced person in black. Then he put on his spectacles and said:

“Prisoners at the bar, you are found guilty of trespass. The punishment is Death﻿—if the judge does not like the prisoners. If he does not dislike them it is imprisonment for life, or until the judge has had time to think it over. Remove the prisoners.”

“Oh, *don’t!*” cried Philip, almost weeping.

===Chinese===
法官坐在高高的青铜宝座上，宝座两侧各有一条巨大的青铜龙，宝座前是又宽又浅的台阶，象牙的，黑白相间。

两名侍从在法官面前的台阶顶上铺开一张圆垫——黄的，很厚——他站起身，向囚犯们敬礼。（「出于对你们不幸的敬意。」卫队长悄声说。）

法官穿一件亮黄色的袍子，系一条绿腰带，他不戴假发，戴一顶形状非常古怪的帽子，从头到尾没摘下来。

审判没有多久。卫队长说得很少，法官说得更少，囚犯们则根本不许开口。法官在一本书里查了点什么，又低声同王室律师和一个穿黑衣裳的酸脸人商量。然后他戴上眼镜，说：

「在庭各犯，你们擅闯罪名成立。刑罚是死刑——如果法官不喜欢这几名囚犯的话。如果他不是不喜欢他们，就是终身监禁，或者关到法官有空把这事想清楚为止。带囚犯下去。」

「哦，*别*！」菲利普喊道，几乎要哭了。

===Original===
“I thought you weren’t afraid,” whispered Lucy.

“Silence in court,” said the judge.

Then Philip and Lucy were removed.

They were marched by streets quite different from those they had come by, and at last in the corner of a square they came to a large house that was quite black.

“Here we are,” said the captain kindly. “Goodbye. Better luck next time.”

The gaoler, a gentleman in black velvet, with a ruff and a pointed beard, came out and welcomed them cordially.

===Chinese===
「我还以为你不害怕呢。」露西小声说。

「庭内肃静。」法官说。

然后菲利普和露西就被带下去了。

押他们走的街道，跟来时的完全不同，最后，在广场一角，他们来到一栋通体乌黑的大房子前。

「到了。」卫队长和气地说。「再见。祝你们下回运气好些。」

狱卒（the gaoler）出来了。他是一位穿黑天鹅绒的绅士，戴着皱领，留着尖胡子，热热络络地欢迎他们。

===Original===
“How do you do, my dears?” he said. “I hope you’ll be comfortable here. First-class misdemeanants, I suppose?” he asked.

“Of course,” said the captain.

“Top floor, if you please,” said the gaoler politely, and stood back to let the children pass. “Turn to the left and up the stairs.”

The stairs were dark and went on and on, and round and round, and up and up. At the very top was a big room, simply furnished with a table, chairs, and a rocking-horse. Who wants more furniture than that?

===Chinese===
「你们好吗，亲爱的孩子们？」他说。「希望你们在这儿住得舒坦。一等轻罪犯人，对吧？」他问。

「当然。」卫队长说。

「请上顶楼。」狱卒客客气气地说，退后一步让孩子们过去。「左转，上楼。」

楼梯黑黢黢的，走啊走，转啊转，上啊上。最顶上是一个大房间，陈设简单：一张桌子，几把椅子，还有一匹摇木马。谁还稀罕比这更多的家具呢？

===Original===
“You’ve got the best view in the whole city,” said the gaoler, “and you’ll be company for me. What? They gave me the post of gaoler because it’s nice, light, gentlemanly work, and leaves me time for my writing. I’m a literary man, you know. But I’ve sometimes found it a trifle lonely. You’re the first prisoners I’ve ever had, you see. If you’ll excuse me I’ll go and order some dinner for you. You’ll be contented with the feast of reason and the flow of soul, I feel certain.”

The moment the door had closed on the gaoler’s black back Philip turned on Lucy.

“I hope you’re satisfied,” he said bitterly. “This is all *your* doing. They’d have let me off if you hadn’t been here. What on earth did you want to come here for? Why did you come running after me like that? You know I don’t like you?”

“You’re the hatefullest, disagreeablest, horridest boy in all the world,” said Lucy firmly﻿—“there!”

Philip had not expected this. He met it as well as he could.

“I’m not a little sneak of a white mouse squeezing in where I’m not wanted, anyhow,” he said.

===Chinese===
「你们这间，是全城景致最好的，」狱卒说，「你们还可以给我作伴。怎么？他们派我当狱卒，是因为这差事清闲、光鲜、合绅士身份，还能腾出工夫搞我的写作。我是文人，你知道的。不过我有时也觉得有几分冷清。你们是我当差以来的头两名囚犯。失陪一下，我去给你们安排晚饭。我确信，你们一定会满足于理智的盛宴和心灵的清谈。」

狱卒黑黑的背影一出门，菲利普就冲着露西发作了。

「这下你满意了吧。」他刻薄地说。「这全是*你*闹出来的。要不是你在这儿，他们早放了我了。你到底跑来干什么？你干吗那样追着我跑？你明知道我不喜欢你！」

「你是全世界最可恨、最讨人嫌、最讨厌的男孩。」露西斩钉截铁地说——「就是这样！」

菲利普没料到这一手。他尽力招架了过去。

「反正呀，我不是那种没人待见还要往里钻的小白鼠。」他说。

===Original===
And then they stood looking at each other, breathing quickly, both of them.

“I’d rather be a white mouse than a cruel bully,” said Lucy at last.

“I’m not a bully,” said Philip.

Then there was another silence. Lucy sniffed. Philip looked round the bare room, and suddenly it came to him that he and Lucy were companions in misfortune, no matter whose fault it was that they were imprisoned. So he said:

“Look here, I don’t like you and I shan’t pretend I do. But I’ll call it Pax for the present if you like. We’ve got to escape from this place somehow, and I’ll help you if you like, and you may help me if you can.”

“Thank you,” said Lucy, in a tone which might have meant anything.

===Chinese===
然后两个人站在那里，你瞪着我，我瞪着你，都喘着粗气。

「我宁可当小白鼠，也不当狠心的恶霸。」露西终于说。

「我不是恶霸。」菲利普说。

接着又是一阵沉默。露西抽了抽鼻子。菲利普环顾这间光秃秃的屋子，忽然明白过来：不管坐牢怪谁，他和露西都是同病相怜的难友。于是他说：

「听着，我不喜欢你，我也不会装作喜欢。可要是你愿意，眼下我就把这叫作 Pax（拉丁语「和平」，两个孩子讲和的约定语）吧。我们总得想法子逃出这个地方，你若愿意，我帮你；你若帮得上，也可以帮我。」

「谢谢你。」露西说，那口气听着什么意思都可能。

===Original===
“So we’ll call it Pax and see if we can escape by the window. There might be ivy﻿—or a faithful page with a rope ladder. Have you a page at the Grange?”

“There’s two stable-boys,” said Lucy, “but I don’t think they’re faithful, and I say, I think all this is much more magic than you think.”

“Of course I know it’s magic,” said he impatiently; “but it’s quite real too.”

“Oh, it’s real enough,” said she.

They leaned out of the window. Alas, there was no ivy. Their window was very high up, and the wall outside, when they touched it with their hand, felt smooth as glass.

“*That’s* no go,” said he, and the two leaned still farther out of the window looking down on the town. There were strong towers and fine minarets and palaces, the palm trees and fountains and gardens. A white building across the square looked strangely familiar. Could it be like St. Paul’s which Philip had been taken to see when he was very little, and which he had never been able to remember? No, he could not remember it even now. The two prisoners looked out in a long silence. Far below lay the city, its trees softly waving in the breeze, flowers shining in a bright many-coloured patchwork, the canals that intersected the big squares gleamed in the sunlight, and crossing and recrossing the squares and streets were the people of the town, coming and going about their business.

===Chinese===
「那我们就说定了 Pax，看看能不能从窗户逃出去。说不定有常春藤——或者有个忠心的侍童带绳梯来。格兰奇庄园有侍童吗？」

「有两个马童，」露西说，「可我觉得他们不忠心。还有，我说，这一切比你以为的可魔法多了。」

「我当然知道这是魔法，」他不耐烦地说；「可它也十足是真的呀。」

「哦，真倒是真的。」她说。

他们探身到窗外。唉，没有常春藤。他们的窗子非常高，窗外的墙，他们伸手一摸，光滑得像玻璃。

「*那*可走不通。」他说。两个人越发探身窗外，俯瞰脚下的城。有坚固的塔楼、漂亮的宣礼塔和宫殿，有棕榈树、喷泉和花园。广场对面一栋白色的建筑，看着眼熟得蹊跷。它会像圣保罗大教堂（St. Paul’s）吗——菲利普很小的时候被人带去见过、后来却怎么也想不起来的那一座？不，就连现在他也想不起来。两名囚犯久久地沉默着望向窗外。下方远处躺着全城，树木在微风里轻轻摇曳，花木织成一片明晃晃的彩色拼花，纵横穿过大广场的运河在日光里闪闪发亮，而在广场和街道上来来往往、穿来穿去的，是城里办着各自事情的居民。

===Original===
“Look here!” said Lucy suddenly, “do you mean to say you don’t know?”

“Know what?” he asked impatiently.

“Where we are. What it is. Don’t you?”

“No. No more do you.”

“Haven’t you seen it all before?”

“No, of course I haven’t. No more have you.”

===Chinese===
「喂！」露西忽然说，「你是说，你竟然不知道？」

「知道什么？」他不耐烦地问。

「我们在哪儿。这是什么。你不知道？」

「不知道。你也不知道。」

「以前没见过这全部的景象吗？」

「没有，当然没有。你也没有。」

===Original===
“All right. I *have* seen it before though,” said Lucy, “and so have you. But I shan’t tell you what it is unless you’ll be nice to me.” Her tone was a little sad, but quite firm.

“I *am* nice to you. I told you it was Pax,” said Philip. “Tell me what you think it is.”

“I don’t mean that sort of grandish standoffish Pax, but real Pax. Oh, don’t be so horrid, Philip. I’m dying to tell you﻿—but I won’t if you go on being like you are.”

“*I’m* all right,” said Philip; “out with it.”

“No. You’ve got to say it’s Pax, and I will stand by you till we get out of this, and I’ll always act like a noble friend to you, and I’ll try my best to like you. Of course if you can’t like me you can’t, but you ought to try. Say it after me, won’t you?”

Her tone was so kind and persuading that he found himself saying after her, “I, Philip, agree to try and like you, Lucy, and to stand by you till we’re out of this, and always to act the part of a noble friend to you. And it’s real Pax. Shake hands.”

===Chinese===
「好。可*我*是见过的，」露西说，「你也见过。可你要是不对我好点儿，我就不告诉你这是什么。」她的口气有点伤心，却十分坚决。

「我*是*对你好。我说了 Pax 的。」菲利普说。「告诉我，你觉得这是什么。」

「我说的不是那种端架子的 Pax，是真正的 Pax。哦，别这么讨厌嘛，菲利普。我憋得要命想告诉你——可你要是还像现在这样，我就偏不说。」

「*我*没怎么样啊，」菲利普说；「快讲吧。」

「不。你得跟着我说：Pax；说我要陪着你，直到我们脱身；说我要永远像一位高贵的朋友那样待你，还要尽最大的努力喜欢你。当然，你要是实在喜欢不上我，那也没办法，可你总该试一试。跟着我说一遍，好不好？」

她的声音那么和善、那么劝得人动心，他发现自己竟跟着她说了起来：「我，菲利普，同意试着喜欢你，露西，陪着你直到我们脱身，并且永远像一位高贵的朋友那样待你。这是真正的 Pax。握手吧。」

===Original===
“Now then,” said he when they had shaken hands, and Lucy uttered these words:

“Don’t you see? It’s your own city that we’re in, your own city that you built on the tables in the drawing-room? It’s all got big by magic, so that we could get in. Look,” she pointed out of the window, “see that great golden dome, that’s one of the brass finger-bowls, and that white building’s my old model of St. Paul’s. And there’s Buckingham Palace over there, with the carved squirrel on the top, and the chessmen, and the blue and white china pepper-pots; and the building we’re in is the black Japanese cabinet.”

Philip looked and he saw that what she said was true. It *was* his city.

“But I didn’t build insides to my buildings,” said he; “and when did *you* see what I built anyway?”

“The insides are part of the magic, I suppose,” Lucy said; “and I saw the cities you built when Auntie brought me home last night, after you’d been sent to bed. And I did love them. And oh, Philip, I’m so glad it’s Pax because I do think you’re so *frightfully* clever, and Auntie thought so too, building those beautiful things. And I knew nurse was going to pull it all down. I begged her not to, but she was addymant, and so I got up and dressed and came down to have another look by moonlight. And one or two of the bricks and chessmen had fallen down. I expect nurse knocked them down. So I built them up again as well as I could﻿—and I was loving it all like anything; and then the door opened and I hid under the table, and you came in.”

===Chinese===
「那么现在——」两人握过手，他说。露西说出了下面这些话：

「你还不明白吗？我们待的这座城，就是你自己的城，你在客厅桌子上搭的那座城啊。它被魔法变大了，好让我们能进来。瞧，」她指向窗外，「看见那个金色的大圆顶了吧，那是一只黄铜洗指碗；那栋白楼，是我的圣保罗大教堂旧模型。那边是白金汉宫（Buckingham Palace），顶上雕着松鼠，还有那些棋子，那些青花瓷胡椒罐；我们脚底下这栋楼，是黑色的日本橱柜。」

菲利普望了望，看出她说的是真的。这*确实*是他的城。

「可我没给房子搭里面呀，」他说；「再说了，*你*是什么时候见过我搭的东西的？」

「里面嘛，我猜也是魔法的一部分，」露西说；「昨晚姑妈把你打发上床以后，把我接了回家，我就看见你搭的城了。我真爱它们。哦，菲利普，我太高兴讲 Pax 了，因为我当真觉得你*特别特别*聪明，能搭出那些漂亮东西来，姑妈也这么想。我就知道保姆要把它们全拆掉。我求她别拆，可她死也不肯（addymant），所以我起了床，穿好衣裳，下楼想在月光里再看一眼。有一两块积木和棋子倒了，我猜是保姆碰倒的，我就尽量把它们重新搭好——我喜欢得不得了；然后门开了，我就躲到桌子底下，你进来了。」
===Original===
“Then you were there﻿—did you notice how the magic began?”

“No, but it all changed to grass; and then I saw you a long way off, going up a ladder. And so I went after you. But I didn’t let you see me. I knew you’d be so cross. And then I looked in at the guardroom door, and I did so want some of the coconut milk.”

“When did you find out it was *my* city?”

“I thought the soldiers looked like my lead ones somehow. But I wasn’t sure till I saw the judge. Why he’s just old Noah, out of the Ark.”

“So he is,” cried Philip; “how wonderful! How perfectly wonderful! I wish we weren’t prisoners. Wouldn’t it be jolly to go all over it﻿—into all the buildings, to see what the insides of them have turned into? And all the other people. I didn’t put *them* in.”

“That’s more magic, I expect. But﻿—Oh, we shall find it all out in time.”

===Chinese===
「那么你当时就在那儿——你注意到魔法是怎么开始的吗？」

「没有，只看见一切变成了草地；后来我看见你在老远的地方，往一架梯子上爬。所以我就跟上去了。可我没让你瞧见。我知道你会发火的。然后我从卫兵室门口往里看，我实在太想喝一点椰子汁了。」

「你是什么时候发现这是*我的*城的？」

「我总觉得那些士兵有点儿像我的铅兵。可我一直拿不准，直到看见了法官。哎呀，他不就是方舟里出来的老诺亚嘛。」

「真的是他！」菲利普喊道；「多神奇啊！妙极了！我们要不是囚犯该多好。到全城各处逛逛——钻进每栋房子，看看它们的里面都变成了什么，该多带劲！还有那些别的人。我可*没有*把他们放进去。」

「我猜那也是魔法。不过——哦，我们早晚都会弄明白的。」

===Original===
She clapped her hands. And on the instant the door opened and the gaoler appeared.

“A visitor for you,” he said, and stood aside to let someone else come in, someone tall and thin, with a black hooded cloak and a black half-mask, such as people wear at carnival time.

When the gaoler had shut the door and gone away the tall figure took off its mask and let fall its cloak, showing to the surprised but recognising eyes of the children the well-known shape of Mr. Noah﻿—the judge.

“How do you do?” he said. “This is a little unofficial visit. I hope I haven’t come at an inconvenient time.”

“We’re very glad,” said Lucy, “because you can tell us﻿—”

“I won’t answer questions,” said Mr. Noah, sitting down stiffly on his yellow mat, “but I will tell you something. We don’t know who you are. But I myself think that you may be the Deliverer.”

===Chinese===
她拍起手来。门应声而开，狱卒出现了。

「有人来探视你们。」他说，退到一旁，让另一个人进来——那人又高又瘦，披一件黑色连帽斗篷，戴一副黑色半截面具，就像人们狂欢节戴的那种。

狱卒关上门走后，那高高的身影摘下面具，褪下斗篷，露出孩子们又惊又认得的那个熟识身形——诺亚先生（Mr. Noah），法官。

「你们好。」他说。「这是一次小小的非正式来访。希望我没有来得不是时候。」

「我们太高兴了，」露西说，「因为您可以告诉我们——」

「我不回答问题，」诺亚先生说着，端端正正地在黄垫子上坐下，「不过我可以主动告诉你们一点事。我们不知道你们是谁。但我自己认为，你们可能就是那位解救者。

===Original===
“Both of us,” said Philip jealously.

“One or both. You see the prophecy says that the Destroyer’s hair is red. And your hair is not red. But before I could get the populace to feel sure of, that my own hair would be grey with thought and argument. Some people are so wooden-headed. And I am not used to thinking. I don’t often have to do it. It distresses me.”

The children said they were sorry. Philip added:

“Do tell us a little about your city. It isn’t a question. We want to know if it’s magic. That isn’t a question either.”

“I was about to tell you,” said Mr. Noah, “and I will not answer questions. Of course it is magic. Everything in the world is magic, until you understand it.

“And as to the city. I will just tell you a little of our history. Many thousand years ago all the cities of our country were built by a great and powerful giant, who brought the materials from far and wide. The place was peopled partly by persons of his choice, and partly by a sort of self-acting magic rather difficult to explain. As soon as the cities were built and the inhabitants placed here the life of the city began, and it was, to those who lived it, as though it had always been. The artisans toiled, the musicians played, and the poets sang. The astrologers, finding themselves in a tall tower evidently designed for such a purpose, began to observe the stars and to prophesy.”

===Chinese===
「把我们俩都算上。」菲利普忙不迭地说。

「一个，或者两个。你知道，预言里说，毁灭者（the Destroyer）的头发是红的。而你们的头发不红。可要等我把老百姓一个个都说信了这一点，我自己的头发都要为思考和争辩熬成灰的了。有些人脑袋就是木头做的。而我呢，不习惯思考。我不常需要思考。一思考我就难受。」

孩子们说他们很抱歉。菲利普又加了一句：

「给我们讲讲你们的城吧。这不是提问。我们想知道它是不是魔法。这也不是提问。」

「我正要讲呢，」诺亚先生说，「而且我不回答问题。它当然是魔法。世上的一切都是魔法，直到你把它弄懂为止。

「至于这座城嘛。我只讲一点点我们的历史。几千年前，我们国家所有的城，都是由一位伟大而强大的巨人建造的，材料是他从四面八方运来的。这地方住的人，一部分出自他的挑选，另一部分出自一种自己会动的魔法，那个不太好解释。城一建好、居民一安顿，城的日子就开始了，而且对住在城里的人来说，仿佛它从来就是这样的。工匠劳作，乐师奏乐，诗人唱歌。占星家们发现自己身处一座高塔之中，那塔明摆着就是为这种用途设计的，于是他们开始观测星象，做出预言。」

===Original===
“I know that part,” said Philip.

“Very well,” said the judge. “Then you know quite enough. Now I want to ask a little favour of you both. Would you mind escaping?”

“If we only could,” Lucy sighed.

“The strain on my nerves is too much,” said Mr. Noah feelingly. “Escape, my dear children, to please me, a very old man in indifferent health and poor spirits.”

“But how﻿—”

“Oh, you just walk out. You, my boy, can disguise yourself in your dressing-gown which I see has been placed on yonder chair, and I will leave my cloak for you, little girl.”

===Chinese===
「这段我知道。」菲利普说。

「很好，」法官说。「那你知道得够多了。现在我想请你们两位帮个小忙。你们愿意越狱吗？」

「要是能越就好了。」露西叹了口气。

「我的神经实在吃不消了，」诺亚先生深有感触地说。「越狱吧，亲爱的孩子们，就当讨我一个高兴——一个身子骨不佳、心气儿不高的老头子。」

「可是怎么——」

「哦，你们走出门就是了。你嘛，我的孩子，可以化装一下，穿上你的晨衣，我看见它已经搁在那边椅子上了；我把我的斗篷留给你，小姑娘。」

===Original===
They both said “Thank you,” and Lucy added: “But *how*?”

“Through the door,” said the judge. “There is a rule about putting prisoners on their honour not to escape, but there have not been any prisoners for so long that I don’t suppose they put you on honour. No? You can just walk out of the door. There are many charitable persons in the city who will help to conceal you. The front-door key turns easily, and I myself will oil it as I go out. Goodbye﻿—thank you so much for falling in with my little idea. Accept an old man’s blessing. Only don’t tell the gaoler. He would never forgive me.”

He got off his mat, rolled it up and went.

“Well!” said Lucy.

“Well!” said Philip.

“I suppose we go?” he said. But Lucy said, “What about the gaoler? Won’t he catch it if we bolt?”

===Chinese===
两人都说了「谢谢您」，露西又问：「可是*怎么*出去呢？」

「从门出去呀。」法官说。「按规定，囚犯要凭荣誉担保不越狱，可是这么久没有囚犯，我看他们没让你们凭荣誉担保吧？没有？那你们只管走出门去就是。城里有不少乐善好施的人，会帮你们藏起来的。大门钥匙很好转，我出去的时候亲手给它上点油。再见——多谢你们肯顺着我这点小小的主意。请受老头子一个祝福。只是千万别告诉狱卒。他永远不会原谅我的。」

他下了垫子，卷起来，走了。

「哎呀！」露西说。

「哎呀！」菲利普说。

「我看，我们走吧？」他说。可露西说：「那狱卒怎么办？我们要是跑了，他不会挨罚吗？」

===Original===
Philip felt this might be true. It was annoying, and as bad as being put on one’s honour.

“Bother!” was what he said.

And then the gaoler came in. He looked pale and worried.
